# Conserved co-abundance modules: brain and lung

## Module 0

**Tissue pair**: brain and lung  
**Genes (45)**: HIBADH, SLC3A2, SLC1A5, GOT2, ACO2, AIFM1, ATP2A2, ATP5F1A, ATP5F1B, ATP5F1C, CRAT, ALCAM, CS, CMC1, CPT2, ECHS1, TUFM, ETFA, TBRG4, QRSL1, GNA11, HADH, HMGCL, IDH3A, PPA2, SLC7A5, ME2, IMMT, NECTIN2, PIGS, PITRM1, UQCRC1, UQCRC2, RTN4, SDHA, SFXN1, SUCLG1, SUCLG2, SUPV3L1, AARS2, IARS2, SARS2, TIMM50, TRAP1, VDAC2

### Function

The module is a conserved mitochondrial energy-metabolism and respiratory module. Primary global evidence shows strong enrichment for the mitochondrial matrix and mitochondrion (GO:CC mitochondrial matrix; q_global = 1.05e-14) (GO:CC mitochondrion; q_global = 2.39e-13), cellular and aerobic respiration, generation of precursor metabolites and energy, and oxidation of organic compounds (4 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 1.56e-05–0.000264; see the table). The module also contains a coherent tricarboxylic acid cycle and carbon-metabolism core (KEGG Carbon metabolism - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 0.000317) (GO:BP tricarboxylic acid cycle; q_global = 0.000353) (KEGG Citrate cycle (TCA cycle) - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 0.000353), respiratory-chain and oxidative-phosphorylation components (4 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 0.000317–0.0391; see the table), fatty-acid and organic-acid catabolic enzymes, mitochondrial translation machinery, and amino-acid transporters (6 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 0.00295–0.0337; see the table). Thus, the best-supported interpretation is coordinated mitochondrial oxidative metabolism, linking substrate entry and catabolism to electron transport and ATP production. Disease-labelled pathway terms should be interpreted as overlaps with mitochondrial energy biology rather than as evidence that the module specifically causes those diseases (5 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 0.000509–0.0322; see the table).

**Cell composition.** This is not primarily a plasma or secreted-protein module and does not resemble a blood-cell module. HPA reports no broad secretory pattern, only one secreted gene, and low immune-cell specificity for most genes. Protein detection is widespread in brain and nearly widespread in lung. The HPA tissue pattern instead points toward metabolically active tissues and cell types, including muscle, liver, tongue, heart, hepatocytes, parietal cells, and enterocytes. A small subset has immune-cell enhancement, including SFXN1, IDH3A, SLC1A5, SLC7A5, CMC1, QRSL1, NECTIN2, and RTN4, but this is not sufficient to make blood contamination the leading explanation. The shared brain-lung signal is therefore more consistent with a common mitochondrial and metabolically active cellular program, while contributions from shared epithelial, muscle, or other metabolically active cell types remain possible.

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING supports a dense, biologically coherent mitochondrial network. Established relationships include the ATP synthase subunit cluster involving ATP5F1A, ATP5F1B, and ATP5F1C; the respiratory-chain cluster involving SDHA, UQCRC1, and UQCRC2; and extensive links among the TCA-cycle and substrate-oxidation enzymes ACO2, CS, IDH3A, SUCLG1, SUCLG2, ETFA, ECHS1, HADH, and related proteins. The amino-acid transporter relationships among SLC1A5, SLC3A2, and SLC7A5 are also supported. These known interactions indicate that the co-abundance structure recapitulates real mitochondrial pathways rather than being a random gene grouping. hu.MAP independently supports ATP synthase, complex III, mitochondrial translation, substrate oxidation, amino-acid transport, and mixed mitochondrial metabolic complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_13468.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10473.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10525.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_14442.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_07245.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09307.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_11095.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09333.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09691.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08546.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09600.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08750.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08716.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_07476.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09775.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09904.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_12621.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10623.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_12457.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_06247.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09592.1). Some module genes have no hu.MAP or STRING relationship, so the module is broader than any single physical complex and should be interpreted as a coordinated organelle-level program rather than one discrete complex.

### Novelty

The mitochondrial interpretation itself is not a new biological discovery: most central relationships, ATP synthase components, respiratory-chain proteins, TCA enzymes, and mitochondrial metabolic complexes are already established by STRING, hu.MAP, and the supplied gene annotations. The potentially novel result is the conserved co-abundance module shared specifically by brain and lung, including the particular combination of oxidative phosphorylation, TCA metabolism, fatty-acid and amino-acid handling, mitochondrial translation, and transport genes. That claim remains a discovery about cross-tissue co-abundance rather than proof of a new physical complex or new molecular function. Absence of a gene pair from STRING or hu.MAP cannot establish novelty, because those resources capture known interactions and complexes incompletely. Replication in independent cohorts, control for cell composition, and comparison with existing cross-tissue co-expression modules would be needed to establish whether the brain-lung conservation is genuinely novel.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module represents a shared mitochondrial metabolic state in metabolically demanding or metabolically active cells of brain and lung. Coordinated abundance of TCA-cycle enzymes, fatty-acid and branched-chain substrate catabolic enzymes, respiratory-chain proteins, ATP synthase subunits, mitochondrial translation factors, and amino-acid transporters may maintain substrate oxidation, respiratory electron transfer, proton-gradient utilization, and ATP supply. SLC1A5, SLC3A2, SLC7A5, and SFXN1 could couple extracellular or cytosolic amino-acid availability to mitochondrial metabolism, while ETFA, CPT2, HADH, ECHS1, and CRAT could connect lipid and acyl-CoA oxidation to the respiratory chain. This is a mechanistic model, not evidence that the module drives disease or that all genes operate in one physical assembly.

### Important genes

#### ATP5F1A

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): ATP5F1C (0.90), CS (0.89), ATP5F1B (0.87), ETFA (0.86), ACO2 (0.86), UQCRC1 (0.85), GOT2 (0.82), SFXN1 (0.80), ME2 (0.80); STRING partners: ACO2 (0.884), ATP5F1B (0.999), ATP5F1C (0.999), CS (0.94), ETFA (0.786), IDH3A (0.855), IMMT (0.809), PPA2 (0.857), SDHA (0.972), SUCLG1 (0.853), TUFM (0.967), UQCRC1 (0.97), UQCRC2 (0.986), VDAC2 (0.715)
- **HPA**: Tissue enhanced; Cell type enhanced; blood Low immune cell specificity; location Mitochondria; IHC detected in brain, lung
- **hu.MAP 3.0**: huMAP3_10473.1, huMAP3_07245.1, huMAP3_09307.1, huMAP3_11095.1, huMAP3_08750.1
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: ATP synthase F1 subunit alpha. This gene encodes a subunit of mitochondrial ATP synthase. Mitochondrial ATP synthase catalyzes ATP synthesis, using an electrochemical gradient of protons across the inner membrane during oxidative phosphorylation. ATP synthase is composed of two linked multi-subunit complexes: the soluble catalytic core, F1, and the membrane-spanning component, Fo, comprising the proton channel. The catalytic po…
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: mitochondrial complex V (ATP synthase) deficiency, nuclear type 4A (0.655); mitochondrial proton-transporting ATP synthase complex deficiency (0.648); mitochondrial complex V (ATP synthase) deficiency, nuclear type 4B (0.532); hereditary disease (0.394); Mitochondrial encephalopathy (0.371)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 822
- **Why important**: ATP5F1A is a highly connected module gene and links the ATP synthase subunit cluster to CS, ACO2, ETFA, SDHA, UQCRC1, UQCRC2, and transport-related genes. It is present across the mitochondrial, respiration, oxidative-phosphorylation, membrane-complex, and energy-related primary terms (27 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 1.05e-14–0.0484; see the table). Its established function is as a catalytic F1 ATP synthase subunit, and its disease associations include mitochondrial complex V deficiency and mitochondrial encephalopathy. It also has substantial literature support.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence identifies ATP5F1A as a central oxidative-phosphorylation and ATP-production component of this module. Its network position supports a coordinating role within the measured co-abundance program, but does not establish that it regulates the other genes.

#### CS

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): ATP5F1A (0.89), GOT2 (0.85), ATP5F1B (0.84), SFXN1 (0.84), ATP5F1C (0.83), SUCLG1 (0.82), UQCRC1 (0.82), ME2 (0.81), ETFA (0.80); STRING partners: ACO2 (0.999), ATP5F1A (0.94), ATP5F1B (0.917), ATP5F1C (0.726), CRAT (0.759), ECHS1 (0.843), GOT2 (0.952), HADH (0.817), HMGCL (0.916), IARS2 (0.702), IDH3A (0.925), ME2 (0.975), SDHA (0.984), SUCLG1 (0.929), SUCLG2 (0.948), UQCRC1 (0.71), UQCRC2 (0.746)
- **HPA**: Tissue enhanced; Low cell type specificity; blood Low immune cell specificity; location Mitochondria; IHC detected in brain, lung
- **hu.MAP 3.0**: no complex with >= 2 module members
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: citrate synthase. The protein encoded by this gene is a Krebs tricarboxylic acid cycle enzyme that catalyzes the synthesis of citrate from oxaloacetate and acetyl coenzyme A. The enzyme is found in nearly all cells capable of oxidative metablism. This protein is nuclear encoded and transported into the mitochondrial matrix, where the mature form is found. [provided by RefSeq, Jul 2008].
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: neurodegenerative disease (0.385); autoimmune disorder of central nervous system (0.251); psoriasis (0.176); deafness (0.107); hearing loss, autosomal recessive (0.107)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 189739
- **Why important**: CS is among the most connected genes and has strong STRING relationships with ACO2, GOT2, IDH3A, ME2, SDHA, SUCLG1, SUCLG2, and ATP synthase subunits. It is a direct TCA-cycle enzyme, appears in the mitochondrial matrix, respiration, carbon-metabolism, and citrate-cycle primary terms (13 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 1.05e-14–0.0448; see the table), and has very extensive literature support. Its HPA profile is enhanced in skeletal muscle and tongue, consistent with metabolically active tissue rather than secretion or blood contamination.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence makes CS a strong metabolic-core marker connecting carbon entry into the TCA cycle with respiratory energy production. Its high connectivity is consistent with pathway coordination, although co-abundance alone cannot demonstrate flux control by CS.

#### SDHA

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): AIFM1 (0.90), IARS2 (0.84), ETFA (0.82), ME2 (0.82), ATP5F1C (0.81), ATP2A2 (0.81); STRING partners: ACO2 (0.975), ATP5F1A (0.972), ATP5F1B (0.917), ATP5F1C (0.75), CS (0.984), ETFA (0.706), GOT2 (0.981), IDH3A (0.819), SUCLG1 (0.999), SUCLG2 (0.997), TRAP1 (0.78), TUFM (0.734), UQCRC1 (0.968), UQCRC2 (0.99)
- **HPA**: Tissue enhanced; Cell type enhanced; blood Low immune cell specificity; location Mitochondria; IHC detected in brain, lung
- **hu.MAP 3.0**: no complex with >= 2 module members
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: succinate dehydrogenase complex flavoprotein subunit A. This gene encodes a major catalytic subunit of succinate-ubiquinone oxidoreductase, a complex of the mitochondrial respiratory chain. The complex is composed of four nuclear-encoded subunits and is localized in the mitochondrial inner membrane. Mutations in this gene have been associated with a form of mitochondrial respiratory chain deficiency known as Leigh Syndrome. A pseudogene has been identi…
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: mitochondrial complex II deficiency, nuclear type 1 (0.813); pheochromocytoma/paraganglioma syndrome 5 (0.790); dilated cardiomyopathy 1GG (0.768); neurodegeneration with ataxia and late-onset optic atrophy (0.761); gastrointestinal stromal tumor (0.743)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 12756
- **Why important**: SDHA is a catalytic component of mitochondrial complex II and connects the TCA cycle directly to the respiratory chain. It has strong STRING relationships with ACO2, CS, GOT2, SUCLG1, SUCLG2, UQCRC1, UQCRC2, and ATP synthase subunits, and is represented across nearly all of the module's core mitochondrial and respiration terms (30 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 1.05e-14–0.0484; see the table). HPA shows enhancement in heart and skeletal muscle and cell-type enhancement in several neuronal and glial populations. Its disease associations include complex II deficiency and neurodegenerative and cardiac disorders, with substantial literature support.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence identifies SDHA as a bridge between TCA metabolism and respiratory electron transfer, making it one of the most informative functional anchors of the module. A disease association should not be interpreted as evidence that this module is pathological in the sampled tissues.

#### ETFA

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): IDH3A (0.87), ATP5F1A (0.86), IARS2 (0.85), CPT2 (0.84), SDHA (0.82), PPA2 (0.81), TIMM50 (0.81), TBRG4 (0.81), SUCLG1 (0.80), CS (0.80); STRING partners: ATP5F1A (0.786), ATP5F1C (0.715), ECHS1 (0.851), HADH (0.791), SDHA (0.706), SUCLG1 (0.956), TUFM (0.734), UQCRC2 (0.757)
- **HPA**: Tissue enhanced; Cell type enhanced; blood Low immune cell specificity; location Mitochondria; IHC detected in brain, lung
- **hu.MAP 3.0**: no complex with >= 2 module members
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: electron transfer flavoprotein subunit alpha. ETFA participates in catalyzing the initial step of the mitochondrial fatty acid beta-oxidation. It shuttles electrons between primary flavoprotein dehydrogenases and the membrane-bound electron transfer flavoprotein ubiquinone oxidoreductase. Defects in electron-transfer-flavoprotein have been implicated in type II glutaricaciduria in which multiple acyl-CoA dehydrogenase deficiencies result in l…
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: multiple acyl-CoA dehydrogenase deficiency (0.834); glutaric aciduria (0.756); hereditary disease (0.469); multiple acyl-CoA dehydrogenase deficiency, mild type (0.370); multiple acyl-CoA dehydrogenase deficiency, severe neonatal type (0.370)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 1433
- **Why important**: ETFA has the highest network prominence in the supplied ranking and connects fatty-acid substrate oxidation with respiratory metabolism through relationships to IDH3A, ATP5F1A, CPT2, SDHA, PPA2, SUCLG1, and other beta-oxidation proteins. It is included in the mitochondrial, matrix, respiration, organic-acid and amino-acid metabolism primary terms (15 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 1.05e-14–0.0484; see the table). Its established function is electron transfer from multiple acyl-CoA dehydrogenases to the respiratory chain. It is associated with multiple acyl-CoA dehydrogenase deficiency and glutaric aciduria and has substantial literature support.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports ETFA as a substrate-flexibility connector that can feed fatty-acid and related organic-acid oxidation into mitochondrial respiration. Its central network position supports this interpretation, while the proposed cross-talk with amino-acid transport remains a hypothesis.

#### SLC1A5

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): SLC3A2 (0.86), TBRG4 (0.83), SLC7A5 (0.82), TIMM50 (0.81); STRING partners: SLC3A2 (0.894), SLC7A5 (0.885)
- **HPA**: Low tissue specificity; Cell type enhanced; blood Immune cell enhanced; location Plasma membrane; IHC detected in brain, lung
- **hu.MAP 3.0**: huMAP3_10473.1, huMAP3_07245.1, huMAP3_09904.1
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: solute carrier family 1 member 5. The SLC1A5 gene encodes a sodium-dependent neutral amino acid transporter that can act as a receptor for RD114/type D retrovirus (Larriba et al., 2001 [PubMed 11781704]).[supplied by OMIM, Jan 2011].
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: neurodegenerative disease (0.463); substance abuse (0.231); attention deficit-hyperactivity disorder (0.231); autoimmune disorder of central nervous system (0.213); hypotensive disorder (0.188)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 5038
- **Why important**: SLC1A5 provides a non-mitochondrial transport entry point into the module. It is linked in STRING and hu.MAP to SLC3A2 and SLC7A5 and appears in the globally significant amino-acid transport, transmembrane transport, and membrane-complex terms (11 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 0.00295–0.0472; see the table). Its established function includes sodium-dependent neutral amino-acid and glutamine transport. HPA shows protein detection in brain and lung and enhancement in selected epithelial and immune-related cell types, so it may also report cellular composition.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence places SLC1A5 at the interface between amino-acid availability and the mitochondrial metabolic program. It is an important module member because it supplies a plausible substrate-transport arm, but the data do not establish that SLC1A5 directly controls oxidative phosphorylation in these tissues.

## Module 1

**Tissue pair**: brain and lung  
**Genes (38)**: ORM1, SERPINA1, A1BG, LRG1, A2M, ALB, APOA1, APOA2, LPA, APOC3, APOD, CP, CFB, CFH, CLU, C3, C7, DCTN5, EXOC5, SERPING1, IGHM, IGKC, ITIH4, KNG1, MINK1, MYH11, BGN, DCN, RBP4, SPTA1, SPTB, TNS1, TF, GC, VTN, VWA1, VWF, AZGP1

### Function

The module is best interpreted as a circulating extracellular protein and lipoprotein module with strong complement, humoral immune, protease-regulatory, lipid-transport, and hemostasis-related features. The primary evidence is the global enrichment of blood microparticle (GO:CC blood microparticle; q_global = 1.57e-15), extracellular space (GO:CC extracellular space; q_global = 3.88e-09), complement and coagulation cascades (KEGG Complement and coagulation cascades - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 6.03e-09), extracellular vesicle-related compartments (5 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 1.77e-08–3.49e-08; see the table), platelet alpha-granule compartments (GO:CC platelet alpha granule lumen; q_global = 1.37e-06) and (GO:CC platelet alpha granule; q_global = 6.72e-06), humoral immune response (GO:BP humoral immune response; q_global = 3.87e-06), complement activation (GO:BP complement activation; q_global = 4.87e-05), lipoprotein particles (GO:CC spherical high-density lipoprotein particle; q_global = 0.00037), immune processes (GO:BP regulation of immune system process; q_global = 0.000481), alternative-pathway complement activation (GO:BP complement activation, alternative pathway; q_global = 0.000879), protease-inhibitor activity (GO:MF endopeptidase inhibitor activity; q_global = 0.0022) and (GO:MF peptidase inhibitor activity; q_global = 0.0022), reverse cholesterol transport (GO:BP reverse cholesterol transport; q_global = 0.00293), and lipoprotein remodeling (24 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 0.00394–0.0412; see the table). The coherent interpretation is not a brain- or lung-intrinsic neuronal or pulmonary pathway, but a shared extracellular blood-protein signal combining hepatic secretory output, innate immune surveillance, lipid transport, and vascular or coagulation biology. The module topology contains a dense core around albumin, apolipoproteins, complement proteins, ceruloplasmin, transferrin, and acute-phase proteins, with smaller branches involving extracellular matrix, vascular, erythrocyte, immunoglobulin, and vesicle-associated proteins. The absence of a single intracellular pathway does not weaken the module: its biology is predominantly extracellular and systemic.

**Cell composition.** The module is overwhelmingly composed of secreted or extracellular proteins, with the HPA summary identifying most genes as secreted and many as secreted to blood or extracellular-matrix proteins. HPA tissue evidence is dominated by liver and hepatocyte enrichment, while brain and lung protein detection is limited for many members. This strongly suggests that the conserved brain-lung co-abundance pattern may reflect shared exposure to circulating plasma proteins, blood microparticles, vascular material, or common sample-associated cell types rather than coordinated expression by resident brain and lung cells. The blood microparticle result (GO:CC blood microparticle; q_global = 1.57e-15), extracellular-space and vesicle results (GO:CC extracellular space; q_global = 3.88e-09) and (5 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 1.77e-08–3.49e-08; see the table), and STRING secreted, extracellular, and blood-microparticle annotations (STRING Keyword Secreted; within-query FDR = 3.29e-23) and (STRING COMPARTMENTS Extracellular region; within-query FDR = 2.14e-16) (STRING COMPARTMENTS Extracellular space; within-query FDR = 2.14e-16) (STRING COMPARTMENTS Blood microparticle; within-query FDR = 2.14e-16) support this interpretation. There are also smaller signals from immune cells, neutrophils, monocytes, B cells, erythrocyte progenitors, endothelial cells, smooth muscle cells, fibroblasts, and pericytes. These may represent genuine shared vascular or stromal biology, but they also make blood contamination or variable blood content an important alternative explanation. The module should therefore not be described as a purely brain-lung tissue program.

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING supports a biologically coherent plasma-protein network rather than an arbitrary co-abundance grouping. Strong known associations connect complement components, including C3, CFB, and CFH; complement regulators such as SERPING1 and CLU; protease and acute-phase proteins such as A2M and SERPINA1; carrier and lipid proteins such as ALB, APOA1, APOA2, APOC3, GC, and RBP4; and vascular or matrix proteins such as VTN and VWF. The STRING enrichment results independently identify plasma-protein studies, secretion, extracellular space, blood microparticles, glycoproteins, and complement or coagulation-related plasma profiles (15 supplementary STRING terms; within-query FDR 5.23e-24–8.92e-16). hu.MAP shows repeated overlap with several complexes dominated by circulating complement, coagulation, acute-phase, carrier, and lipoprotein proteins, especially complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_15040.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10678.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09531.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10101.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08395.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_07265.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_07432.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09434.1). The recurring membership of C3, CFB, SERPINA1, A2M, KNG1, APOA1, APOA2, APOC3, ORM1, ALB, GC, TF, SERPING1, CFH, and ITIH4 indicates that much of the module corresponds to already recognized biochemical or proteomic groupings. The smaller branches involving BGN, DCN, VWA1, MYH11, SPTA1, SPTB, MINK1, DCTN5, and EXOC5 have weaker or absent STRING and hu.MAP support and may reflect tissue, cellular, or co-abundance-specific structure.

### Novelty

This module is unlikely to be a wholly new molecular pathway. Its central biology is strongly anticipated by established plasma proteomics, complement, coagulation, lipoprotein, extracellular-vesicle, and hu.MAP relationships. In particular, repeated STRING associations and recurring hu.MAP complexes show that many core relationships were already known. The potentially novel finding is the conservation of this particular co-abundance pattern across brain and lung and the way it combines a hepatic plasma-protein signature with smaller vascular, matrix, erythrocyte, and tissue-associated branches. That cross-tissue conservation could be useful as a systems-level observation, but it does not by itself establish a new physical complex or a brain-lung-specific mechanism. Absence of hu.MAP or STRING support for some peripheral genes is evidence of limited prior interaction annotation, not proof of novelty. Validation should include adjustment for blood content, vascular fraction, liver-derived protein carryover, and cell-type composition, together with independent tissue cohorts and targeted protein measurements.

**Hypothesis.** Mechanistic hypothesis: variation in the abundance or delivery of circulating liver-derived proteins and blood-derived extracellular particles produces a shared extracellular milieu detectable in both brain and lung samples. Within that milieu, complement activation and its regulation interact with protease inhibitors, lipoproteins, iron and vitamin carriers, extracellular matrix proteins, platelet or vascular components, and vesicle-associated cargo. This could couple innate immune tone, lipid and retinoid transport, vascular barrier or matrix state, and tissue stress across the two organs. The hypothesis predicts that module activity will track plasma exposure, vascular or blood-microparticle content, complement and acute-phase status, and possibly inflammatory or vascular injury states. It remains a hypothesis: the co-abundance data and annotations do not demonstrate that these proteins physically act together in resident brain or lung cells, nor that the module causes disease.

### Important genes

#### C3

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): SERPING1 (0.87), CP (0.85), VWA1 (0.84), CFB (0.82); STRING partners: ALB (0.959), APOA1 (0.957), CFB (0.999), CFH (0.999), CLU (0.803), CP (0.751), GC (0.768), ITIH4 (0.745), KNG1 (0.804), ORM1 (0.775), SERPINA1 (0.859), SERPING1 (0.954), VTN (0.79)

- **hu.MAP 3.0**: huMAP3_15040.1, huMAP3_10678.1, huMAP3_09531.1, huMAP3_10101.1, huMAP3_08395.1, huMAP3_07432.1, huMAP3_09434.1, huMAP3_08224.1, huMAP3_02242.1
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: complement C3. Complement component C3 plays a central role in the activation of complement system. Its activation is required for both classical and alternative complement activation pathways. The encoded preproprotein is proteolytically processed to generate alpha and beta subunits that form the mature protein, which is then further processed to generate numerous peptide products. The C3a peptide, also known a…
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: atypical hemolytic-uremic syndrome with C3 anomaly (0.798); complement component 3 deficiency (0.771); age-related macular degeneration (0.740); complement 3 glomerulopathy (0.596); atypical hemolytic-uremic syndrome (0.594)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 153947
- **Why important**: C3 is a central network member of the complement branch and is linked in STRING to CFB, CFH, SERPING1, C7-related biology, carrier proteins, and several plasma proteins. It appears across many primary terms, including complement activation, alternative-pathway activation, humoral immunity, immune response, extracellular compartments, and the KEGG complement and coagulation pathway (KEGG Complement and coagulation cascades - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 6.03e-09), (GO:BP humoral immune response; q_global = 3.87e-06), (GO:BP complement activation; q_global = 4.87e-05), (GO:BP complement activation, alternative pathway; q_global = 0.000879), and (GO:BP regulation of complement activation; q_global = 0.00466). Its disease associations include complement deficiency, complement-mediated renal disease, and age-related macular degeneration. It also has substantial literature support. Its importance is therefore both topological and mechanistic.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports C3 as a core marker and likely functional anchor of the module's complement and extracellular immune signature. A possible role in coordinating the brain-lung shared signal through local complement activity is a hypothesis, because the HPA evidence favors liver and circulating protein contribution and does not establish resident tissue production.

The protein encoded by this gene is a serine protease inhibitor belonging to the serpin superfamily whose targets include elastase, plasmin, thrombin, trypsin, chymotrypsin, and plasminogen activator. This protein is produced in the liver, the bone marrow, by lymphocytic and monocytic cells in lymphoid tissue, and by the Paneth cells of the gut. Defects in this gene are associated with chronic obs…
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: alpha 1-antitrypsin deficiency (0.768); chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (0.690); cirrhosis of liver (0.573); cholelithiasis (0.569); pulmonary emphysema (0.569)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 6526
- **Why important**: SERPINA1 has a prominent position in the module and connects the complement, acute-phase, protease-inhibitor, extracellular-vesicle, and plasma-protein components. It is included in the complement and coagulation pathway (KEGG Complement and coagulation cascades - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 6.03e-09), multiple extracellular and granule terms, and the peptidase-inhibitor terms (GO:MF endopeptidase inhibitor activity; q_global = 0.0022), (GO:MF peptidase inhibitor activity; q_global = 0.0022), and (GO:MF endopeptidase regulator activity; q_global = 0.00428). HPA identifies liver enrichment, blood secretion, vesicle localization, and enrichment among several myeloid cell types. Its established disease associations include alpha-one-antitrypsin deficiency, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, emphysema, and liver disease, making it especially relevant to the lung side of the tissue pair. Its literature is substantial.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports SERPINA1 as a major circulating protease-control and inflammatory-state marker that could connect plasma composition with pulmonary vulnerability. It should not be interpreted as proof of a lung-resident mechanism, because its liver and blood secretion profile makes systemic exposure an equally strong explanation.

## Module 2

**Tissue pair**: brain and lung  
**Genes (13)**: AATF, BOP1, CWC27, LAS1L, MATR3, NUP98, RAE1, TAF15, DPF2, POLR2D, ALYREF, TPX2, WDR36

### Function

The strongest interpretation is a conserved nuclear gene-expression and RNA-processing module. Both globally corrected GO terms support a nuclear-lumen location (GO:CC nuclear lumen; q_global = 0.00679) and participation in nuclear protein-containing complexes (GO:CC nuclear protein-containing complex; q_global = 0.00817). The module combines RNA polymerase II transcription machinery (POLR2D and TAF15), nucleocytoplasmic RNA export (NUP98, RAE1 and ALYREF), pre-mRNA splicing (CWC27), ribosome and rRNA biogenesis (AATF, BOP1, WDR36 and LAS1L), nuclear structural or RNA regulatory functions (MATR3 and DPF2), and cell-cycle spindle biology (TPX2). The STRING enrichment for RNA metabolism (STRING RCTM Metabolism of RNA; within-query FDR = 0.0037) is supplementary and converges with this interpretation, but the primary global evidence is (GO:CC nuclear lumen; q_global = 0.00679) and (GO:CC nuclear protein-containing complex; q_global = 0.00817).

**Cell composition.** This is not a plasma-protein or secreted-protein module: no member is annotated as secreted, and the proteins are predominantly nuclear or nucleolar. The HPA data show broad or low tissue specificity for many members, with several tissue-enhanced signals in testis, bone marrow, skeletal muscle and lymphoid tissue. Cell-type enhancement is frequent, especially in germ-cell, progenitor and selected epithelial populations, but immune-cell specificity is generally low and only TPX2 is immune-cell enhanced. Protein detection in brain and lung is widespread among the tested members, supporting biological presence in both tissues. The pattern is therefore more consistent with a broadly used nuclear housekeeping or proliferative program, potentially modulated by cell composition, than with a blood-derived or secreted signal. The absence of enrichment for a GO or KEGG term would not by itself invalidate a module; here, however, the nuclear terms do pass global correction.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 2 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

| Term | q_global | genes |
|---|---|---|
| GO:CC nuclear lumen | 0.00679 | AATF, ALYREF, BOP1, CWC27, DPF2, LAS1L, MATR3, NUP98, POLR2D, RAE1, TAF15, TPX2, WDR36 |
| GO:CC nuclear protein-containing complex | 0.00817 | AATF, BOP1, CWC27, DPF2, LAS1L, NUP98, POLR2D, RAE1, WDR36 |

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING supports a connected but selective set of known relationships. The strongest links include the NUP98–RAE1 export relationship, the ALYREF–RAE1 connection, and the AATF–WDR36 and BOP1–WDR36 connections. STRING also links ALYREF with CWC27 and NUP98, and AATF with BOP1. These relationships indicate that part of the co-abundance structure recapitulates established RNA-export, nuclear, and ribosome-biogenesis biology rather than representing an entirely uncharacterized interaction system. The STRING functional results for nuclear lumen, nuclear complexes, nucleus, nucleoplasm, RNA metabolism and ribonucleoprotein complexes (8 supplementary STRING terms; within-query FDR 0.0026–0.0258) are concordant supplementary evidence, not global enrichment claims. hu.MAP independently places NUP98 and RAE1 together in several nuclear pore, RNA-export and nucleocytoplasmic-transport complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_03647.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_06369.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10000.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_12581.1). The fact that most other module members do not appear together in the reported hu.MAP complexes suggests that the module is a co-abundance program spanning several related nuclear processes, rather than one single canonical complex.

**STRING interactions (score >= 0.7)**: 7 of 78 gene pairs: AATF–BOP1 (0.753); AATF–WDR36 (0.998); ALYREF–CWC27 (0.814); ALYREF–NUP98 (0.74); ALYREF–RAE1 (0.942); BOP1–WDR36 (0.951); NUP98–RAE1 (0.999)
**STRING functional enrichment (supplementary, within-query FDR)**: Component Nuclear lumen (FDR = 0.0026, 13 genes); Component Nuclear protein-containing complex (FDR = 0.0026, 9 genes); RCTM Metabolism of RNA (FDR = 0.0037, 8 genes); COMPARTMENTS Nucleus (FDR = 0.0044, 13 genes); COMPARTMENTS Nuclear lumen (FDR = 0.0044, 10 genes); Component Nucleoplasm (FDR = 0.007, 12 genes); Keyword Nucleus (FDR = 0.0141, 12 genes); Component Ribonucleoprotein complex (FDR = 0.0258, 6 genes)
**hu.MAP 3.0 complexes with >= 2 module members**: huMAP3_03647.1 (NUP98, RAE1; 2/6); huMAP3_06369.1 (NUP98, RAE1; 2/12); huMAP3_10000.1 (NUP98, RAE1; 2/44); huMAP3_12581.1 (NUP98, RAE1; 2/58)

### Novelty

The module should not be claimed as a wholly new molecular pathway. Several of its relationships and functions are already established by STRING, hu.MAP, curated gene annotations and the literature, particularly the NUP98–RAE1 nuclear-export axis and the RNA-processing and ribosome-biogenesis components. Nevertheless, the specific conserved brain–lung co-abundance organization may represent a new systems-level observation, because the supplied resources do not show one unified complex containing all members. Absence from STRING or hu.MAP alone is not evidence of novelty; novelty would require independent replication, comparison with existing co-expression studies, and testing whether the module persists after accounting for cell composition and technical effects.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module reflects coordinated regulation of nuclear gene expression in cells shared between brain and lung, or in a broadly distributed cell program represented in both tissues. A possible mechanism is that transcriptional activity and RNA polymerase II function are coupled to splicing, RNA export through the NUP98–RAE1 axis, and ribosome biogenesis, with DPF2 and MATR3 supporting chromatin or nuclear RNA regulation and TPX2 marking a proliferative or cell-cycle-linked component. The HPA pattern raises the alternative possibility that the conserved signal is partly driven by shared progenitor, epithelial, or other cell-state composition rather than by a brain–lung specific mechanism. This hypothesis requires perturbation experiments and cell-resolved replication.

## Module 3

**Tissue pair**: brain and lung  
**Genes (18)**: ABHD10, CC2D1A, CDC50A, CCDC93, HSD17B8, IQGAP2, L2HGDH, ALDH6A1, MROH1, NADK2, PCCB, PI4KA, RAB18, RMDN3, RNASET2, STX6, SUCLA2, TLN2

### Function

No GO/KEGG term passed global correction, so the function cannot be assigned from statistically significant pathway enrichment. The strongest biological signal is a mitochondrial and intermediary-metabolism theme: PCCB, L2HGDH, ALDH6A1, SUCLA2, NADK2 and HSD17B8 have annotations involving propionyl-CoA, branched-chain amino-acid, organic-acid, tricarboxylic-acid-cycle, redox or steroid and fatty-acid metabolism. This interpretation is supported only in part by the supplementary STRING disease association (STRING DISEASES Amino acid metabolic disorder; within-query FDR = 0.031), which links several members to amino-acid metabolic disorder and should not be treated as globally corrected enrichment. A second theme concerns membrane trafficking, vesicle biology, phospholipid handling and cytoskeletal organization, represented by STX6, CDC50A, PI4KA, CCDC93, RAB18, IQGAP2, TLN2 and RMDN3. The module therefore appears functionally mixed rather than a sharply defined canonical pathway. Its structure is compatible with coordinated cellular metabolic state and organelle or membrane trafficking, but the absence of globally significant GO/KEGG terms makes that interpretation provisional.

**Cell composition.** The module is not predominantly composed of plasma or secreted proteins: only RNASET2 is annotated as secreted to blood. It is also not dominated by blood-cell-enriched genes. Most members have low immune-cell specificity, although a smaller subset is immune-cell enhanced, including genes associated with basophils, eosinophils or neutrophils. The HPA tissue profile is mixed, with many genes showing low tissue specificity and others enhanced in liver, kidney, brain, skeletal muscle or thyroid gland. Many members are cell-type enhanced, especially in proximal tubule cells, neutrophil-related populations, germ-cell populations, ependymal cells and hepatocytes. This breadth argues against a simple blood-contamination explanation, while leaving open the possibility that shared cell populations or common cellular states contribute to the brain-lung conservation. Protein detection is broad in both tissues, supporting genuine tissue presence rather than a signal confined to one organ. The HPA pattern does not support calling this a housekeeping module, but it does support caution because several genes have strong enrichment outside brain and lung.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 0 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING provides one high-confidence interaction within the module, ALDH6A1–PCCB. This is consistent with the metabolic interpretation and gives the metabolic subnetwork a directly supported relationship. The other co-abundance edges are not represented as high-confidence STRING interactions in the fact sheet, so they should not be described as established physical interactions. hu.MAP identifies IQGAP2 and SUCLA2 together in (hu.MAP huMAP3_10273.1) and (hu.MAP huMAP3_10665.1), but (hu.MAP huMAP3_10665.1) is a very broad complex with many other proteins and only a small subset represented in the module. PCCB and HSD17B8 occur together in the large hu.MAP complex (hu.MAP huMAP3_13468.1), which provides contextual support for a metabolic or organelle-associated association but is not specific evidence that the entire module is one complex. Overall, the external interaction resources validate selected relationships while also showing that most of the module's topology is not already explained by known protein complexes or high-confidence physical interactions.

**STRING interactions (score >= 0.7)**: 1 of 153 gene pairs: ALDH6A1–PCCB (0.967)
**STRING functional enrichment (supplementary, within-query FDR)**: DISEASES Amino acid metabolic disorder (FDR = 0.031, 5 genes)
**hu.MAP 3.0 complexes with >= 2 module members**: huMAP3_10273.1 (IQGAP2, SUCLA2; 2/3); huMAP3_10665.1 (IQGAP2, SUCLA2; 2/42); huMAP3_13468.1 (PCCB, HSD17B8; 2/99)

### Novelty

The module is best described as a candidate novel cross-tissue co-abundance pattern, not as a proven new biological pathway. Its particular combination of mitochondrial metabolic genes, membrane-trafficking genes and cytoskeletal or signaling genes is not fully recapitulated by the single STRING interaction or by the partial hu.MAP overlaps. That mismatch supports potential novelty at the level of coordinated abundance in brain and lung. However, absence of the other module edges from STRING or hu.MAP does not prove novelty, because database coverage, context dependence and detection thresholds can hide real relationships. The lack of globally significant GO/KEGG enrichment also prevents a strong claim that this is a newly discovered coherent functional module. A defensible conclusion is that the conserved co-abundance structure is potentially novel and merits replication, cell-composition adjustment, and validation with independent cohorts and perturbation or biochemical experiments.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module reflects a shared organelle-coupled cellular state in which mitochondrial carbon and amino-acid metabolism is coordinated with membrane trafficking and phospholipid remodeling. PCCB, L2HGDH, ALDH6A1, SUCLA2, NADK2 and HSD17B8 could represent the metabolic component, while PI4KA, STX6, CDC50A, CCDC93, RAB18, IQGAP2, TLN2 and RMDN3 could reflect membrane, vesicle, cytoskeletal or calcium-linked processes. RNASET2 could add an extracellular or innate-immune-associated component, although that connection is speculative. The shared signal might arise from metabolically active epithelial, neural, glial or other organelle-rich cells present in both tissues, or from a common response state rather than direct physical coupling among all module proteins. This hypothesis is not established by the enrichment results, because no GO/KEGG term passed global correction.

- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports PCCB as a central metabolic marker and a key network anchor. It is plausible, but not established, that PCCB helps organize the broader module-level association between mitochondrial metabolism and membrane processes.

## Module 4

**Tissue pair**: brain and lung  
**Genes (13)**: ABRAXAS2, CLIP2, EXOC1, FNTA, KLC2, NDUFA6, PPP1R9B, OCRL, PIP4K2B, PIK3C3, PPP1R21, UPF2, SEPTIN6

### Function

The strongest biological interpretation is a membrane-trafficking and phosphoinositide-regulation module, with links to endosomal processing, autophagy, cytoskeletal organization, and vesicle transport. This interpretation is supported by the functions of OCRL, PIP4K2B, PIK3C3, EXOC1, CLIP2, KLC2, PPP1R9B, and PPP1R21. OCRL regulates phosphoinositides and membrane trafficking; PIP4K2B and PIK3C3 act in phosphoinositide metabolism and autophagy-related pathways; EXOC1 supports exocytic vesicle docking; and CLIP2, KLC2, PPP1R9B, and PPP1R21 connect the module to microtubules, vesicles, or intracellular organization. No GO or KEGG term passed global correction, so the module-level functional interpretation is based on gene functions, network structure, and supplementary STRING evidence rather than globally significant enrichment. The STRING phosphoinositide and inositol-phosphate terms are supplementary evidence only.

**Cell composition.** The module is not composed of plasma or secreted proteins: the HPA summary reports no secreted genes. Most genes have low tissue specificity, and most have low immune-cell specificity, so the composition does not strongly indicate a blood-cell module or a generic plasma-protein signal. There is, however, clear cell-type structure. Brain-associated expression is represented by genes enriched or enhanced in brain, oligodendrocytes, neurons, astrocytes, or choroid plexus epithelial cells, while other genes show lymphoid, monocyte, neutrophil, platelet, or diverse epithelial associations. Protein detection in both brain and lung for most genes is consistent with a shared cellular or intracellular program, but does not by itself establish that the same cell type drives the module in both tissues. Blood contamination is not the leading explanation from the HPA summary, although the cell-type heterogeneity warrants cell-composition-aware validation.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 0 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

### STRING and hu.MAP

The only high-confidence STRING relationship within the module is the OCRL--PIP4K2B pair. This is important because both proteins directly concern phosphoinositide regulation and provides a known molecular anchor for the module. STRING supplementary enrichment connects OCRL, PIP4K2B, and PIK3C3 to phosphoinositide metabolism, inositol-phosphate metabolism, and phosphatidylinositol signaling. The remaining co-abundance links are not supported by high-confidence within-module STRING edges in the fact sheet, so they may reflect indirect coordination, shared cell-state regulation, or context-specific interactions. No hu.MAP complex contains multiple module members. Thus, the module is not a previously recognized stable protein complex; it is better viewed as a coordinated functional neighborhood with one particularly well-supported biochemical connection.

**STRING interactions (score >= 0.7)**: 1 of 78 gene pairs: OCRL–PIP4K2B (0.918)
**STRING functional enrichment (supplementary, within-query FDR)**: WikiPathways Phosphoinositides metabolism (FDR = 0.0154, 3 genes); KEGG Inositol phosphate metabolism (FDR = 0.0205, 3 genes); KEGG Phosphatidylinositol signaling system (FDR = 0.0205, 3 genes)
**hu.MAP 3.0 complexes with >= 2 module members**: none

### Novelty

The module may represent a new cross-tissue co-abundance association, but the evidence does not justify calling it a wholly new biological pathway. Its central phosphoinositide interpretation is already grounded in established gene functions and the known OCRL--PIP4K2B STRING relationship, while PIK3C3 provides a plausible related endolysosomal and autophagy connection. Conversely, the full brain-lung co-abundance configuration has no matching hu.MAP complex and has limited direct STRING support, which leaves room for a novel tissue-shared regulatory pattern. Absence from STRING or hu.MAP alone is not proof of novelty. A claim of novelty should therefore be limited to the specific cross-tissue co-abundance organization, pending replication in independent cohorts and testing against cell-composition and technical-confounding models.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: in shared brain and lung cellular contexts, coordinated regulation of phosphoinositide turnover may organize endosomal and plasma-membrane trafficking, autophagic flux, and microtubule-based vesicle movement. OCRL and PIP4K2B may provide a lipid-regulatory core, with PIK3C3 extending the program toward endosome maturation and autophagy. EXOC1, CLIP2, KLC2, PPP1R9B, and PPP1R21 may couple this lipid state to vesicle docking, microtubule transport, and cytoskeletal scaffolding. This is a mechanistic hypothesis rather than a demonstrated physical complex or causal pathway, because global GO and KEGG enrichment was negative, hu.MAP found no shared complex, and most module edges lack high-confidence STRING support.

## Module 5

**Tissue pair**: brain and lung  
**Genes (10)**: ACADVL, MCAT, GOSR1, MICOS13, NHERF2, DBT, PCK2, SETD7, THYN1, YIPF4

### Function

The module does not have a statistically supported GO or KEGG functional annotation: no GO/KEGG term passed global correction. Its gene content nevertheless points toward a mixed cellular program involving mitochondrial energy and intermediary metabolism, mitochondrial membrane organization, intracellular trafficking, plasma-membrane scaffolding, and nuclear regulation. MCAT, PCK2, DBT, and ACADVL support mitochondrial lipid, gluconeogenic, branched-chain amino-acid, and fatty-acid oxidation biology. MICOS13 supports mitochondrial crista organization, while GOSR1 and YIPF4 support endoplasmic-reticulum-to-Golgi trafficking and vesicle fusion. SETD7 and THYN1 provide nuclear and stress-response connections. Because these annotations are heterogeneous and no term was globally enriched, the most defensible interpretation is a conserved co-abundance program of general cellular metabolism and organelle maintenance rather than a narrowly defined pathway.

**Cell composition.** The module is not dominated by secreted proteins, since the HPA summary reports no secreted genes. Most genes have low tissue specificity, and most have low immune-cell specificity, arguing against a module composed primarily of plasma proteins or blood-cell markers. Several genes show cell-type-enhanced rather than tissue-restricted expression, including respiratory ciliated, respiratory secretory, endothelial, hepatocyte, cardiomyocyte, and reproductive cell annotations. PCK2 is group-enriched in intestine, kidney, and liver, and NHERF2 is tissue-enhanced in heart muscle. The broad detection of nearly all members in brain and lung supports reproducible protein presence in both tissues, but the cell-type annotations mean that shared cellular composition or broadly distributed housekeeping-like biology could contribute to the cross-tissue signal. The available evidence does not support interpreting this module as a blood-contamination signature.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 0 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

### STRING and hu.MAP

The co-abundance network contains relationships among all module members, with MCAT acting as the main local connector and additional links joining MICOS13 with DBT and SETD7, GOSR1 with YIPF4, and YIPF4 with ACADVL. However, none of the possible within-module gene pairs has a high-confidence STRING interaction in the supplied analysis, and STRING functional enrichment is absent. No hu.MAP complex contains multiple module members. Thus, the module is supported as a co-abundance pattern but not as a known physical protein complex or a STRING-established interaction network. The absence of STRING and hu.MAP support may indicate context-specific regulation, indirect relationships, incomplete database coverage, or a composite module rather than a single molecular machine.

**STRING interactions (score >= 0.7)**: 0 of 45 gene pairs.
**hu.MAP 3.0 complexes with >= 2 module members**: none

### Novelty

This appears to be a potentially novel cross-tissue co-abundance pattern, because it has no matching high-confidence STRING interactions, no multi-member hu.MAP complex, and no globally significant GO or KEGG term. Those observations do not by themselves prove biological novelty: the genes individually have established functions, and database absence can reflect incomplete coverage or context dependence. The stronger novelty claim is therefore limited to the particular brain-lung co-abundance organization and its apparent combination of mitochondrial, trafficking, membrane-scaffold, and nuclear genes. Independent replication, preservation in additional cohorts, cell-type-aware analysis, and targeted measurement of the module would be needed before calling it a new biological mechanism.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module may represent a shared organelle-maintenance and metabolic-support state present in selected brain and lung cell populations. Mitochondrial lipid handling, branched-chain amino-acid catabolism, gluconeogenic flux, fatty-acid oxidation, and crista organization could be coordinated with ER-to-Golgi trafficking and membrane scaffolding to maintain energy supply, membrane composition, and protein transport. MCAT may provide the strongest co-abundance bridge between mitochondrial metabolism and the membrane-associated or trafficking portion of the module. This is a hypothesis rather than an established pathway, because the module lacks globally enriched terms, STRING support, and hu.MAP support.

Detection in brain and lung makes it relevant to the shared tissue context, while its cardiomyocyte-enhanced annotation links the module to high-energy-demand cell biology.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports ACADVL as a key mitochondrial fatty-acid oxidation gene. Its association with the trafficking gene YIPF4 may indicate coordinated organelle or membrane demand, but that interpretation is a hypothesis.

It is detected in brain and lung and has a moderate literature base, making it an important representative of the non-mitochondrial portion of the module.

- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports NHERF2 as a membrane-scaffold and transporter-regulation protein. Its inclusion may reflect cell-type composition or coordinated membrane physiology rather than a shared mitochondrial pathway; a functional bridge to MCAT is not established.

## Module 6

**Tissue pair**: brain and lung  
**Genes (30)**: ACIN1, ACSF3, AMPD2, ATRX, ATXN3, BCLAF1, DDI2, ADAR, EHMT1, EPS15L1, CLINT1, FAM120A, FIP1L1, GGA1, IGF2BP3, MTIF2, PPP1R12A, NBN, NUP214, PRRC2A, PRPF31, PUM1, PPAT, RBM22, SEC16A, SNW1, SRSF5, WDR18, WDR3, ZFR

### Function

The best-supported interpretation is a conserved nuclear gene-expression module centered on nucleoplasmic biology. The primary evidence is enrichment for the nucleoplasm (GO:CC nucleoplasm; q_global = 0.0218), with broad overlap across genes involved in RNA binding, pre-mRNA splicing, RNA processing, chromatin regulation, DNA damage responses, nuclear transport, and ribosome-related nuclear functions. The STRING annotations support RNA processing, RNA binding, spliceosome biology, RNA metabolism, and gene expression (STRING Keyword mRNA processing; within-query FDR = 0.0164)(STRING Keyword RNA-binding; within-query FDR = 0.0164)(STRING RCTM Metabolism of RNA; within-query FDR = 0.0306)(STRING KEGG Spliceosome; within-query FDR = 0.0327)(STRING Process Gene expression; within-query FDR = 0.0423), but these are supplementary rather than globally corrected evidence. The module therefore appears to represent coordinated abundance of nuclear RNA-processing and gene-regulatory machinery rather than a single narrowly defined pathway. Its presence in both brain and lung is consistent with shared cellular requirements for nuclear RNA maturation and genome maintenance.

**Cell composition.** This is not a secreted-protein module: HPA identifies no secreted genes. Most genes have low tissue specificity and low immune-cell specificity, and protein detection is broadly present in both brain and lung. This argues against the module being primarily a plasma-protein or blood-contamination signal. Nevertheless, several genes show enhancement in neutrophils, monocytes, progenitors, or other specialized cell types, so contribution from shared or variable cell populations cannot be excluded. The overall composition is more consistent with broadly expressed intracellular housekeeping and gene-regulatory machinery, with some cell-state and lineage-specific modulation, than with a module dominated by blood cells.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 1 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

| Term | q_global | genes |
|---|---|---|
| GO:CC nucleoplasm | 0.0218 | ACIN1, ADAR, ATRX, ATXN3, BCLAF1, CLINT1, DDI2, EHMT1, FIP1L1, GGA1, MTIF2, NBN, NUP214, PPP1R12A, PRPF31, PRRC2A, PUM1, RBM22, SNW1, SRSF5, WDR18, WDR3 |

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING provides direct high-confidence support for selected relationships: RBM22 with SNW1, CLINT1 with PUM1, ATRX with NBN and BCLAF1, BCLAF1 with SNW1, and WDR18 with WDR3. These links independently reinforce spliceosome-related, chromatin or DNA-maintenance, RNA-binding, and nuclear-complex interpretations, but most co-abundance edges do not have a corresponding STRING interaction in the supplied evidence. hu.MAP provides stronger complex-level context. ADAR, PUM1, ZFR, and FAM120A recur in RNA-binding complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_10625.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_07115.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_09223.1), SNW1 and RBM22 recur in spliceosome-associated complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_06429.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_03019.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_08703.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_06726.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_08501.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_09504.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_09945.1), BCLAF1 and ACIN1 recur in RNA-processing complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_11073.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_06097.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_10564.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_09943.1), and ATRX with EHMT1 occurs in a chromatin-associated complex (hu.MAP huMAP3_11539.1). CLINT1, EPS15L1, and SEC16A recur in vesicle-trafficking complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_10762.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_12918.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_12394.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_05120.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_08744.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_05849.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_08320.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_09403.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_10876.1). Thus, the module contains several known molecular neighborhoods rather than one experimentally established physical complex.

**STRING interactions (score >= 0.7)**: 6 of 435 gene pairs: ATRX–BCLAF1 (0.769); ATRX–NBN (0.796); BCLAF1–SNW1 (0.823); CLINT1–PUM1 (0.892); RBM22–SNW1 (0.999); WDR18–WDR3 (0.801)

**hu.MAP 3.0 complexes with >= 2 module members**: huMAP3_10625.1 (ADAR, PUM1, ZFR, FAM120A; 4/57); huMAP3_07115.1 (PUM1, ZFR, FAM120A; 3/15); huMAP3_09223.1 (PUM1, ZFR, FAM120A; 3/15); huMAP3_10762.1 (SEC16A, CLINT1, EPS15L1; 3/62); huMAP3_12918.1 (SEC16A, CLINT1, EPS15L1; 3/62); huMAP3_11073.1 (FIP1L1, BCLAF1, ACIN1; 3/67); huMAP3_12394.1 (SEC16A, CLINT1, EPS15L1; 3/98); huMAP3_02335.1 (ZFR, FAM120A; 2/2); huMAP3_00582.1 (PUM1, FAM120A; 2/3); huMAP3_07846.1 (PUM1, FAM120A; 2/6); huMAP3_06192.1 (ZFR, FAM120A; 2/8); huMAP3_05120.1 (CLINT1, EPS15L1; 2/14); huMAP3_09281.1 (ZFR, FAM120A; 2/14); huMAP3_06429.1 (SNW1, RBM22; 2/18); huMAP3_08744.1 (SEC16A, CLINT1; 2/20); huMAP3_03019.1 (SNW1, RBM22; 2/20); huMAP3_05849.1 (CLINT1, EPS15L1; 2/21); huMAP3_08703.1 (SNW1, RBM22; 2/21); huMAP3_06097.1 (BCLAF1, ACIN1; 2/22); huMAP3_08320.1 (CLINT1, EPS15L1; 2/24); huMAP3_09403.1 (CLINT1, EPS15L1; 2/26); huMAP3_11539.1 (ATRX, EHMT1; 2/32); huMAP3_10564.1 (BCLAF1, ACIN1; 2/37); huMAP3_06726.1 (SNW1, RBM22; 2/37); huMAP3_10876.1 (CLINT1, EPS15L1; 2/40); huMAP3_08501.1 (SNW1, RBM22; 2/47); huMAP3_09504.1 (SNW1, RBM22; 2/51); huMAP3_09945.1 (SNW1, RBM22; 2/61); huMAP3_09943.1 (BCLAF1, ACIN1; 2/78); huMAP3_13468.1 (MTIF2, ACSF3; 2/99)

### Novelty

The biological components and several internal relationships are not new: spliceosome, RNA-binding, chromatin, DNA-repair, and vesicle-trafficking roles are already represented by STRING and hu.MAP evidence. However, the specific conserved brain-lung co-abundance module may still be a new systems-level observation because its defining feature is coordinated abundance across tissues, not discovery of every gene or interaction. The limited STRING coverage and partial membership of hu.MAP complexes support possible novelty of the module arrangement, but absence from those resources alone cannot establish novelty. A defensible conclusion is that this is a potentially novel cross-tissue co-abundance organization built largely from known nuclear and cellular-machinery genes, requiring replication in independent cohorts and comparison with published tissue co-expression modules before being called a new biological mechanism.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module reflects a shared cellular program that couples nuclear RNA maturation with chromatin and genome-stability control in brain and lung cells. In this model, spliceosome and RNA-binding components coordinate transcript processing, ADAR and related RNA-binding factors tune transcript structure or stability, while ATRX, EHMT1, NBN, and associated nuclear factors help maintain chromatin and DNA integrity. Vesicle-trafficking genes may represent a connected cellular-state component or a neighboring co-abundance signal rather than the central mechanism. The coordinated abundance could therefore support adaptation to tissue-specific transcriptional demands while preserving RNA quality and nuclear homeostasis. This is a mechanistic hypothesis, not a demonstrated causal pathway.

#### ACIN1

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): EHMT1 (0.91), BCLAF1 (0.87), RBM22 (0.83), SEC16A (0.82), PRRC2A (0.80), SRSF5 (0.80), NBN (0.80); STRING partners: none

- **Function (MyGene.info)**: apoptotic chromatin condensation inducer 1. Apoptosis is defined by several morphologic nuclear changes, including chromatin condensation and nuclear fragmentation. This gene encodes a nuclear protein that induces apoptotic chromatin condensation after activation by caspase-3, without inducing DNA fragmentation. This protein has also been shown to be a component of a splicing-dependent multiprotein exon junction complex (EJC) that is deposi…
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: neurodegenerative disease (0.547); adolescent idiopathic scoliosis (0.090); hepatocellular carcinoma (0.076); acute lymphoblastic leukemia (0.074); pancreatic neoplasm (0.045)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 894
- **Why important**: ACIN1 is among the strongest network-connected genes in the module and links several central neighbors, including EHMT1, BCLAF1, RBM22, PRRC2A, SRSF5, and NBN. It is included in the primary nucleoplasm evidence (GO:CC nucleoplasm; q_global = 0.0218) and in the supplementary RNA-processing and spliceosome annotations (STRING Keyword mRNA processing; within-query FDR = 0.0164)(STRING RCTM Metabolism of RNA; within-query FDR = 0.0306)(STRING KEGG Spliceosome; within-query FDR = 0.0327)(STRING Process Gene expression; within-query FDR = 0.0423). Its established functions include apoptotic chromatin condensation, RNA splicing, and participation in an exon-junction-associated complex. It also has substantial literature and disease-oriented annotation, making it a useful integrator of the module's RNA-processing and nuclear-response signals.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports ACIN1 as a central nuclear RNA-processing and apoptosis-associated component of the module. Its network position makes it an informative hub, but the data do not establish that ACIN1 drives the cross-tissue co-abundance pattern or that it causally links splicing to apoptosis in this module.

- **Conclusion**: Established evidence identifies ADAR as a biologically important RNA-processing and RNA-editing component that plausibly explains part of the module's nuclear RNA signature. It remains a hypothesis that ADAR-mediated editing is functionally responsible for the observed brain-lung co-abundance rather than simply co-varying with broadly expressed nuclear machinery.

## Module 7

**Tissue pair**: brain and lung  
**Genes (38)**: ACLY, MTHFD1, CACYBP, DYNC1I2, DHX9, EIF3F, EIF3L, FKBP4, FLNB, G6PD, GARS1, GCLM, HSPH1, PKM, PLRG1, PSMC2, PSMA1, PSMA4, PAICS, UPF1, SHC1, SUPT16H, SRP68, SRP72, AARS1, DARS1, EPRS1, KARS1, QARS1, TARS1, YARS1, CCT5, CCT3, CCT7, CCT8, TXNRD1, EFTUD2, UBLCP1

### Function

The strongest interpretation is a conserved cytosolic gene-expression and proteostasis module centered on protein synthesis. The clearest primary evidence is the coordinated presence of aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases and aminoacylation-related genes in (GO:MF aminoacyl-tRNA ligase activity; q_global = 8.21e-06), (GO:BP tRNA aminoacylation for protein translation; q_global = 8.21e-06), (GO:BP amino acid activation; q_global = 1.61e-05), (GO:BP tRNA aminoacylation; q_global = 1.61e-05), (KEGG Aminoacyl-tRNA biosynthesis - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 1.61e-05), (GO:BP amino acid metabolic process; q_global = 0.00136), (GO:MF catalytic activity, acting on a tRNA; q_global = 0.00166), (GO:BP tRNA metabolic process; q_global = 0.0143), and (GO:MF catalytic activity, acting on RNA; q_global = 0.0155). This supports a role in charging tRNAs and sustaining translation rather than a tissue-specific signaling pathway. Translation and protein biosynthesis are also supported by (GO:BP translation; q_global = 0.0006) and (GO:BP protein biosynthetic process; q_global = 0.0006), while the CCT3, CCT5, CCT7, and CCT8 group supports ATP-dependent protein folding through the CCT chaperonin in (GO:CC chaperonin-containing T-complex; q_global = 0.000107), (GO:MF ATP-dependent protein folding chaperone; q_global = 0.000757), (GO:MF protein folding chaperone; q_global = 0.0094), and (GO:CC protein folding chaperone complex; q_global = 0.0369). The module additionally contains coherent proteasome, RNA processing, mRNA surveillance, metabolic, redox, and cotranslational targeting components. Thus, the module most plausibly represents coordinated production, folding, quality control, and turnover of proteins. The telomerase and Cajal-body terms are globally significant in (GO:BP regulation of telomerase RNA localization to Cajal body; q_global = 0.000107) through (GO:BP positive regulation of protein localization to Cajal body; q_global = 0.00037) and (GO:BP RNA localization to Cajal body; q_global = 0.000879) through (GO:BP protein localization to nucleoplasm; q_global = 0.000879), but they are driven by the same CCT subunit group and should be interpreted as a possible specialized consequence of chaperonin biology rather than the central module function. The extracellular annotations in (GO:CC extracellular space; q_global = 0.0362) and (GO:CC extracellular region; q_global = 0.0387) are not sufficient to infer a secreted module because most member proteins are intracellular and the HPA data do not support a predominantly secreted composition.

**Cell composition.** This is not predominantly a plasma-protein or secreted-protein module. HPA reports protein detection for every member in both brain and lung, while nearly all genes have low tissue specificity and low immune-cell specificity. Only a small minority are secreted or membrane-associated, and the module is dominated by cytosolic or nuclear housekeeping-type machinery: aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases, translation factors, chaperonins, proteasome components, RNA-processing proteins, and core metabolic enzymes. STRING supplementary tissue terms identify blood, lymphocyte, and lymphoblast associations in (STRING TISSUES Lymphoblast; within-query FDR = 2.05e-14), (STRING TISSUES Lymphocyte; within-query FDR = 9.9e-10), and (STRING TISSUES Blood; within-query FDR = 1.39e-06), but these are compatible with broadly expressed cellular machinery and do not establish blood contamination. HPA identifies only limited immune-cell specificity, with G6PD being immune-cell enhanced for eosinophils. The shared brain-lung signal therefore more likely reflects conserved cellular requirements and shared ubiquitous cell programs than a blood-cell-specific module, although sample cellular composition remains a possible contributor.

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING provides strong prior support for several internal relationships. The aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases form a dense, biologically coherent interaction group involving AARS1, DARS1, EPRS1, GARS1, KARS1, QARS1, TARS1, and YARS1, matching (STRING RCTM Translation; within-query FDR = 4.35e-07), (STRING RCTM Cytosolic tRNA aminoacylation; within-query FDR = 4.44e-07), (STRING Keyword Aminoacyl-tRNA synthetase; within-query FDR = 8.93e-07), (STRING KEGG Aminoacyl-tRNA biosynthesis; within-query FDR = 2e-06), and (STRING Function aminoacyl-tRNA ligase activity; within-query FDR = 2.19e-06). CCT3, CCT5, CCT7, and CCT8 show highly coherent known relationships, and PSMA1, PSMA4, and PSMC2 form a known proteasome-associated group. Other supported pairs include EIF3F–EIF3L, SRP68–SRP72, MTHFD1–PAICS, G6PD–PKM, and GCLM–TXNRD1. hu.MAP independently recovers the aminoacyl-tRNA synthetase complex in (hu.MAP huMAP3_02637.1) and (hu.MAP huMAP3_13688.1), the CCT complex in (hu.MAP huMAP3_07938.1) through (hu.MAP huMAP3_01951.1), proteasome complexes in (hu.MAP huMAP3_09656.1), (hu.MAP huMAP3_13725.1), and (hu.MAP huMAP3_00356.1), translation-initiation complexes in (hu.MAP huMAP3_01276.1) through (hu.MAP huMAP3_11246.1), and additional metabolic, spliceosomal, and RNA-processing complexes. These results indicate that much of the module is composed of established physical or functional assemblies rather than arbitrary co-abundance pairs. At the same time, several module genes lack STRING partners or hu.MAP complexes, so the co-abundance structure may also capture shared regulation, abundance coupling, or indirect physiological coordination beyond physical interaction.

### Novelty

The module should not be described as a wholly new molecular pathway. Its central features reproduce well-established biology: aminoacyl-tRNA synthetase cooperation, CCT chaperonin assembly, proteasome organization, translation initiation, and metabolic coupling are all supported by STRING and hu.MAP. Absence of a relationship from STRING or hu.MAP would not demonstrate novelty, especially for co-abundance rather than physical-interaction data. The potentially novel result is the conservation of a single co-abundance program across brain and lung that links these established subsystems in one tissue-pair module. That integrated cross-tissue organization may be a new observation, but it requires replication in independent cohorts, control for cell composition, and comparison with published co-expression or proteomics modules before claiming discovery. The current evidence supports novelty of the module-level organization as a possibility, not novelty of the constituent functions or complexes.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module is a conserved cellular-resource and proteostasis program that becomes coordinately abundant in brain and lung cells with substantial demands for accurate protein synthesis, folding, trafficking, and quality control. A core aminoacylation unit may supply charged tRNAs for translation; EIF3F, EIF3L, SRP68, and SRP72 may support initiation and targeting of newly synthesized proteins; CCT3, CCT5, CCT7, and CCT8 may fold selected nascent proteins; and PSMA1, PSMA4, and PSMC2 may remove damaged or surplus proteins. PKM, G6PD, ACLY, MTHFD1, PAICS, GCLM, and TXNRD1 could provide energy, nucleotide, acetyl-CoA, folate, purine, and redox support for this workload. UPF1, DHX9, EFTUD2, and PLRG1 could couple the program to RNA surveillance and processing. This is a mechanistic model rather than a demonstrated causal pathway: the co-abundance data establish coordinated abundance, not direct flux, physical assembly, or tissue-specific regulation.

### Important genes

#### CCT8

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): CCT7 (0.95), CCT5 (0.93), CCT3 (0.90), KARS1 (0.90), DARS1 (0.87), UPF1 (0.84), QARS1 (0.83), FKBP4 (0.82), PSMC2 (0.82), DHX9 (0.82), PSMA4 (0.81); STRING partners: CCT3 (0.999), CCT5 (0.999), CCT7 (0.999), PSMA1 (0.797), PSMA4 (0.731), PSMC2 (0.733)
- **HPA**: Low tissue specificity; Cell type enhanced; blood Low immune cell specificity; location Nucleoplasm, Connecting piece, End piece; IHC detected in brain, lung
- **hu.MAP 3.0**: huMAP3_07938.1, huMAP3_06559.1, huMAP3_09191.1, huMAP3_10656.1, huMAP3_14584.1
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: chaperonin containing TCP1 subunit 8. This gene encodes the theta subunit of the CCT chaperonin, which is abundant in the eukaryotic cytosol and may be involved in the transport and assembly of newly synthesized proteins. Alternative splicing results in multiple transcript variants of this gene. A pseudogene related to this gene is located on chromosome 1. [provided by RefSeq, Sep 2013].
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: colorectal carcinoma (0.094); neoplasm (0.093); hepatocellular carcinoma (0.090); lung adenocarcinoma (0.087); cancer (0.083)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 1127
- **Why important**: CCT8 is the highest-positioned node in the module and connects the CCT subnetwork with aminoacylation, RNA surveillance, and proteasome-associated proteins. It belongs to the CCT chaperonin and is represented across the globally corrected folding, cytosol, Cajal-body, telomerase, and translation-related evidence, including (GO:CC chaperonin-containing T-complex; q_global = 0.000107), (GO:MF ATP-dependent protein folding chaperone; q_global = 0.000757), (GO:CC intracellular protein-containing complex; q_global = 0.00587), and (GO:MF protein folding chaperone; q_global = 0.0094). STRING and hu.MAP independently support its relationships with the other CCT subunits and with proteasome components. HPA detects it in both tissues and shows low tissue specificity, consistent with a broadly deployed cellular function. The gene also has extensive literature and cancer-related disease associations in the supplied evidence.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports CCT8 as a central marker of the module's protein-folding and proteostasis arm. Its network position and annotations make it an important integrator, but the data do not establish that CCT8 initiates or controls the entire module. A role in coupling chaperonin activity to the translation and quality-control program remains a hypothesis.

## Module 8

**Tissue pair**: brain and lung  
**Genes (47)**: AKAP8, BRIX1, CCAR1, CHORDC1, COIL, CPSF3, DAZAP1, DDX21, DDX42, DDX46, DDX5, EIF2B4, EIF2A, FMR1, GNL3, GRWD1, HDGF, HNRNPR, EIF4G1, EIF6, MYBBP1A, MRE11, NOP2, NUP93, PLIN3, PRPF4, GART, PYM1, RBM10, RBM39, RCC1, RPL12, RSL1D1, RPL3, RPL6, RPL9, RRS1, RUVBL2, SF3A1, SRRT, TBCE, TCOF1, TCERG1, TIAL1, TRMT2A, YLPM1, ZNF326

### Function

The module is a conserved nuclear RNA and ribonucleoprotein gene-expression module. Primary enrichment supports RNA binding (GO:MF RNA binding; q_global = 8.29e-14), nucleic-acid binding (GO:MF nucleic acid binding; q_global = 1.95e-12), ribonucleoprotein complexes (GO:CC ribonucleoprotein complex; q_global = 1.79e-06), RNA and mRNA processing (GO:BP mRNA processing; q_global = 1.33e-05) (GO:BP RNA processing; q_global = 1.35e-05) (GO:BP mRNA metabolic process; q_global = 0.000173), spliceosomal RNA splicing (GO:BP RNA splicing, via transesterification reactions with bulged adenosine as nucleophile; q_global = 0.000582) (GO:BP mRNA splicing, via spliceosome; q_global = 0.000582) (GO:BP RNA splicing; q_global = 0.000604), protein–RNA complex organization (GO:BP protein-RNA complex assembly; q_global = 0.000498) (GO:BP protein-RNA complex organization; q_global = 0.00086), ribosome assembly (GO:BP ribosome assembly; q_global = 0.00774) (GO:BP ribosomal large subunit assembly; q_global = 0.00941), and translation (GO:BP cytoplasmic translation; q_global = 0.00741) (GO:BP translation; q_global = 0.0232) (GO:BP protein biosynthetic process; q_global = 0.0232). The accompanying nuclear, nucleolar, nuclear-speck, Cajal-body and ribosome annotations (GO:CC nuclear lumen; q_global = 0.00023) (GO:CC nucleolus; q_global = 0.00326) (GO:CC Cajal body; q_global = 0.0182) (GO:CC nuclear speck; q_global = 0.043) indicate a linked pathway spanning nuclear RNA maturation, ribosome biogenesis and delivery of processed transcripts to translation. The signal is biologically coherent rather than dependent on a single annotation family. The subgraph therefore most plausibly represents coordinated RNA metabolism and gene-expression capacity, with both spliceosomal and ribosome-production components.

**Cell composition.** The module is not predominantly composed of plasma or secreted proteins and does not show a blood-cell signature. HPA reports low immune-cell specificity for every module gene and only one secreted gene, HDGF. Most genes have low tissue specificity, with protein detected in both brain and lung for most assessed genes. Several genes are cell-type enhanced in reproductive, epithelial, retinal, glial or progenitor populations, but these patterns do not define the shared module as a blood contaminant. The broad tissue distribution and frequent brain and lung protein detection are consistent with a broadly used intracellular housekeeping-type program, while the shared abundance pattern could still reflect common cellular composition or similar demands for RNA processing and protein synthesis in the sampled tissues.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 47 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING provides extensive support for known relationships within two major subnetworks: a nucleolar and ribosome-biogenesis group involving BRIX1, DDX21, EIF6, GNL3, GRWD1, NOP2, RRS1, RSL1D1 and ribosomal proteins, and a spliceosomal group involving SF3A1, DDX42, DDX46, PRPF4, RBM10, RBM39, TCERG1 and related RNA-binding proteins. These are supplementary STRING findings (15 supplementary STRING terms; within-query FDR 1.34e-16–1.27e-05), not globally corrected enrichment evidence. hu.MAP independently places module members in established spliceosomal and RNA-processing complexes, especially PRPF4, COIL, SF3A1, DDX46 and DDX42 (hu.MAP huMAP3_08980.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_06511.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_04549.1), as well as complexes containing RBM10 and CCAR1 (hu.MAP huMAP3_10359.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_11912.1). Other hu.MAP overlaps connect HNRNPR with ZNF326 (hu.MAP huMAP3_09654.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_07581.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10659.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10625.1), RPL3 with GRWD1 (hu.MAP huMAP3_08465.1), HDGF with RBM10 (hu.MAP huMAP3_11021.1), and PYM1 with SRRT (hu.MAP huMAP3_11073.1). Thus, the network is enriched for relationships that are already known, while the co-abundance module integrates several established complexes in a shared tissue-level pattern.

**STRING interactions (score >= 0.7)**: 83 of 1081 gene pairs: BRIX1–DDX21 (0.709); BRIX1–EIF6 (0.962); BRIX1–GNL3 (0.98); BRIX1–GRWD1 (0.929); BRIX1–MYBBP1A (0.76); BRIX1–NOP2 (0.986); BRIX1–RPL3 (0.871); BRIX1–RPL6 (0.892); BRIX1–RPL9 (0.862); BRIX1–RRS1 (0.977); BRIX1–RSL1D1 (0.997); CCAR1–DDX5 (0.836); CCAR1–FMR1 (0.748); CCAR1–RBM10 (0.743); CCAR1–RBM39 (0.875); CCAR1–SRRT (0.857); CCAR1–TCERG1 (0.92); DDX21–GNL3 (0.963); DDX21–HNRNPR (0.73); DDX21–MYBBP1A (0.963); DDX21–NOP2 (0.761); DDX21–RBM39 (0.724); DDX21–RRS1 (0.842); DDX21–RSL1D1 (0.81); DDX42–SF3A1 (0.903); DDX46–SF3A1 (0.999); DDX46–TCERG1 (0.927); DDX5–HDGF (0.765); DDX5–HNRNPR (0.844); DDX5–RBM39 (0.971); DDX5–SRRT (0.855); DDX5–TCERG1 (0.836); EIF4G1–EIF6 (0.775); EIF4G1–FMR1 (0.865); EIF4G1–TIAL1 (0.905); EIF6–GNL3 (0.947); EIF6–NOP2 (0.948); EIF6–RPL12 (0.858); EIF6–RPL3 (0.99); EIF6–RPL6 (0.996); EIF6–RPL9 (0.993); EIF6–RRS1 (0.812); EIF6–RSL1D1 (0.955); FMR1–TIAL1 (0.904); GART–MYBBP1A (0.971); GART–RPL3 (0.805); GNL3–GRWD1 (0.868); GNL3–NOP2 (0.966); GNL3–RCC1 (0.743); GNL3–RPL3 (0.737); GNL3–RPL6 (0.88); GNL3–RPL9 (0.793); GNL3–RRS1 (0.95); GNL3–RSL1D1 (0.991); GRWD1–MYBBP1A (0.904); GRWD1–NOP2 (0.896); GRWD1–RPL3 (0.985); GRWD1–RRS1 (0.926); GRWD1–RSL1D1 (0.979); MYBBP1A–NOP2 (0.864); MYBBP1A–RSL1D1 (0.831); NOP2–RPL6 (0.915); NOP2–RPL9 (0.766); NOP2–RRS1 (0.944); NOP2–RSL1D1 (0.982); PRPF4–SF3A1 (0.998); RBM10–SF3A1 (0.858); RBM39–SF3A1 (0.722); RBM39–SRRT (0.936); RBM39–TCERG1 (0.887); RPL12–RPL3 (0.999); RPL12–RPL6 (0.999); RPL12–RPL9 (0.999); RPL3–RPL6 (0.999); RPL3–RPL9 (0.999); RPL3–RRS1 (0.75); RPL3–RSL1D1 (0.846); RPL6–RPL9 (0.999); RPL6–RSL1D1 (0.919); RPL9–RSL1D1 (0.858); RRS1–RSL1D1 (0.992); SF3A1–TCERG1 (0.992); SRRT–TCERG1 (0.839)
