### Novelty

This should not be presented as discovery of a new molecular function or a previously unknown physical interaction network. The principal functions are well established, and many relationships are supported by STRING and hu.MAP. The potentially novel result is the conserved co-abundance organization of these RNA-processing, nucleolar and translational components across brain and lung. That observation may be useful as a tissue-pair-level systems finding, but novelty requires independent replication, comparison with published co-expression modules and testing against cellular composition and technical covariates. Absence of a member or edge from STRING or hu.MAP would not establish novelty, particularly because those resources represent incomplete and differently defined evidence.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module reflects a shared intracellular RNA-production and translation-support program in brain and lung cells. A coordinated nucleolar arm may support ribosomal RNA maturation and large-subunit assembly through BRIX1, DDX21, NOP2, RRS1 and associated ribosomal factors, while a nuclear-speck and spliceosomal arm may process and regulate transcripts through SF3A1, DDX42, DDX46, PRPF4, RBM10, RBM39 and TCERG1. The resulting processed transcripts could be transferred into translation through EIF4G1, EIF6, EIF2A, ribosomal proteins and RNA-binding adaptors. This is a mechanistic interpretation of co-abundance, not proof that the proteins form one physical complex or that the module directly causes a tissue phenotype.

### Important genes

#### DDX5

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): RSL1D1 (0.84), RBM39 (0.84), CPSF3 (0.81); STRING partners: CCAR1 (0.836), HDGF (0.765), HNRNPR (0.844), RBM39 (0.971), SRRT (0.855), TCERG1 (0.836)
- **HPA**: Low tissue specificity; Low cell type specificity; blood Low immune cell specificity; location Nucleoplasm, Nucleoli; IHC detected in brain, lung
- **hu.MAP 3.0**: no complex with >= 2 module members
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: DEAD-box helicase 5. This gene encodes a member of the DEAD box family of RNA helicases that are involved in a variety of cellular processes as a result of its role as an adaptor molecule, promoting interactions with a large number of other factors. This protein is involved in pathways that include the alteration of RNA structures, plays a role as a coregulator of transcription, a regulator of splicing, and in the pro…
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: COVID-19 (0.374); severe acute respiratory syndrome (0.371); colon adenocarcinoma (0.370); colorectal adenocarcinoma (0.370); dengue disease (0.370)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 4163
- **Why important**: DDX5 is a central functional bridge between RNA binding, RNA processing, spliceosomal regulation and nuclear RNA granules. It has module links to RSL1D1, RBM39 and CPSF3, and STRING relationships with several splicing and transcriptional regulators (15 supplementary STRING terms; within-query FDR 1.34e-16–1.27e-05). Its annotations support RNA-structure remodeling, transcriptional co-regulation, alternative splicing and small noncoding RNA processing. It also has substantial literature and disease associations including viral disease and cancer.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports DDX5 as a multifunctional RNA-processing and splicing regulator that helps explain the module's nuclear RNA phenotype. Its position and annotations make it a plausible integrator, but the co-abundance data do not establish that DDX5 drives the module or directly coordinates every linked process.

#### SF3A1

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): NUP93 (0.90), RRS1 (0.85), RSL1D1 (0.83), DAZAP1 (0.80); STRING partners: DDX42 (0.903), DDX46 (0.999), PRPF4 (0.998), RBM10 (0.858), RBM39 (0.722), TCERG1 (0.992)
- **HPA**: Low tissue specificity; Cell type enhanced; blood Low immune cell specificity; location Nucleoplasm, Nuclear speckles; IHC detected in brain, lung
- **hu.MAP 3.0**: huMAP3_08980.1, huMAP3_06511.1, huMAP3_10359.1, huMAP3_04549.1
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: splicing factor 3a subunit 1. This gene encodes a subunit of the splicing factor 3a protein complex. The splicing factor 3a heterotrimer is a component of the mature U2 small nuclear ribonucleoprotein particle (snRNP). U2 small nuclear ribonucleoproteins play a critical role in spliceosome assembly and pre-mRNA splicing. [provided by RefSeq, Aug 2014].
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: dengue disease (0.370); diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (0.334); neurodegenerative disease (0.320); type 2 diabetes mellitus (0.126); schizophrenia (0.077)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 1069
- **Why important**: SF3A1 is a core spliceosomal component and has direct module connections to DDX42, DDX46, PRPF4, RBM10, RBM39 and TCERG1. It is present in the strongest hu.MAP spliceosomal complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_08980.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_06511.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10359.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_04549.1) and in essentially every relevant RNA-processing enrichment category, including spliceosome assembly and mRNA splicing (GO:BP mRNA processing; q_global = 1.33e-05) (4 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 0.000582–0.000627; see the table). It has substantial literature and associations with neurodegenerative disease, lymphoma, diabetes and schizophrenia.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence identifies SF3A1 as a strong molecular anchor for the spliceosomal branch of the module. Its co-abundance with nucleolar and translational genes suggests coordinated gene-expression infrastructure, but does not demonstrate direct functional coupling between all branches.

They are implicated in a number of cellular processes involving alteration of RNA secondary structure such as translation initiation, nuclear and mitochondrial splicing, and ribosome and spliceosome assembly. Based on their distribution patterns, some members of this family are believed to b…
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: neurodegenerative disease (0.396); colorectal carcinoma (0.112); cancer (0.099); gastric cancer (0.098); hepatocellular carcinoma (0.095)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 1867
- **Why important**: DDX21 links the nucleolar subnetwork to multiple ribosome-biogenesis and RNA-processing genes, including BRIX1, GNL3, MYBBP1A, NOP2, RRS1 and RSL1D1. Its annotations support RNA helicase activity, ribosome and spliceosome assembly, chromatin remodeling and regulation of gene expression. It is detected in brain and lung and has extensive literature, including associations with neurodegenerative disease and cancer.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports DDX21 as a nucleolar RNA-remodeling and ribosome-biogenesis component that connects major parts of the module. A coordinating role across nucleolar and splicing processes is plausible, but remains a hypothesis from network structure rather than a demonstrated causal mechanism.

#### BRIX1

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): EIF4G1 (0.87), RSL1D1 (0.87), PRPF4 (0.81), TCOF1 (0.80), EIF2A (0.80), DDX21 (0.80); STRING partners: DDX21 (0.709), EIF6 (0.962), GNL3 (0.98), GRWD1 (0.929), MYBBP1A (0.76), NOP2 (0.986), RPL3 (0.871), RPL6 (0.892), RPL9 (0.862), RRS1 (0.977), RSL1D1 (0.997)

- **hu.MAP 3.0**: no complex with >= 2 module members
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: biogenesis of ribosomes BRX1. Enables RNA binding activity. Predicted to be involved in ribosomal large subunit assembly. Located in chromosome and nucleolus. [provided by Alliance of Genome Resources, Apr 2022]
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: colorectal cancer (0.077); cancer (0.077); colorectal carcinoma (0.074); deficiency anemia (0.065); sialolithiasis (0.060)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 251
- **Why important**: BRIX1 is a nucleolar ribosome-biogenesis factor with module links to EIF4G1, RSL1D1, PRPF4, TCOF1, EIF2A and DDX21. It participates in globally enriched RNA-binding, macromolecule biosynthesis, ribonucleoprotein organization, nucleolar localization and large-subunit assembly terms (GO:MF RNA binding; q_global = 8.29e-14) (GO:BP macromolecule biosynthetic process; q_global = 2.66e-06) (GO:BP protein-RNA complex assembly; q_global = 0.000498) (GO:CC nucleolus; q_global = 0.00326) (GO:BP ribosome assembly; q_global = 0.00774) (GO:BP ribosomal large subunit assembly; q_global = 0.00941). STRING connects it strongly with the nucleolar and ribosomal portion of the module, while its disease associations include colorectal cancer and deficiency anemia.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports BRIX1 as a representative of the module's nucleolar and ribosome-assembly arm. Its cross-links to translation and RNA-processing genes make it important for interpreting the module as an integrated gene-expression program, although its causal importance in brain or lung is untested here.

#### RSL1D1

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): RBM39 (0.89), TCOF1 (0.87), BRIX1 (0.87), TBCE (0.86), RPL12 (0.85), TIAL1 (0.84), DDX5 (0.84), GRWD1 (0.84), CPSF3 (0.83), SF3A1 (0.83), DDX42 (0.83), DAZAP1 (0.83), EIF4G1 (0.82), DDX21 (0.82), CCAR1 (0.82), YLPM1 (0.81), PRPF4 (0.81), RCC1 (0.81), CHORDC1 (0.80), EIF2B4 (0.80); STRING partners: BRIX1 (0.997), DDX21 (0.81), EIF6 (0.955), GNL3 (0.991), GRWD1 (0.979), MYBBP1A (0.831), NOP2 (0.982), RPL3 (0.846), RPL6 (0.919), RPL9 (0.858), RRS1 (0.992)
- **HPA**: Low tissue specificity; Low cell type specificity; blood Low immune cell specificity; location Nucleoli rim, Mitotic chromosome; IHC detected in brain, lung
- **hu.MAP 3.0**: no complex with >= 2 module members
- **Function (MyGene.info)**: ribosomal L1 domain containing 1. Enables mRNA 3'-UTR binding activity and mRNA 5'-UTR binding activity. Involved in regulation of apoptotic process and regulation of cellular senescence. Acts upstream of or within regulation of protein localization. Located in chromosome and nucleolus. [provided by Alliance of Genome Resources, Apr 2022]
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: adolescent idiopathic scoliosis (0.219); hepatocellular carcinoma (0.099); neoplasm (0.099); colorectal carcinoma (0.096); insomnia (0.078)
- **Europe PMC papers**: 381
- **Why important**: RSL1D1 has the strongest network position in the supplied module and connects to both nucleolar or ribosomal genes and RNA-binding or translation-related genes. It is annotated for mRNA binding, regulation of apoptosis, cellular senescence and protein localization, and is located at the nucleoli rim and mitotic chromosome. STRING supports numerous relationships with BRIX1, DDX21, EIF6, GNL3, NOP2, RRS1 and ribosomal proteins (STRING Function Nucleic acid binding; within-query FDR = 1.34e-16) (STRING Function RNA binding; within-query FDR = 6.49e-16) (STRING COMPARTMENTS Nuclear lumen; within-query FDR = 3.31e-09) (STRING Process Gene expression; within-query FDR = 2.19e-07) (STRING Component Ribonucleoprotein complex; within-query FDR = 5.26e-07). Its literature and disease associations are more limited than those of several other selected genes.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports RSL1D1 as a highly connected module hub that may stabilize the observed association between nucleolar, ribosomal and RNA-binding components. Its hub status is important for interpretation, but network centrality and co-abundance alone do not establish a specific biochemical mechanism.

## Module 9

**Tissue pair**: brain and lung  
**Genes (37)**: APMAP, ATP1A1, ATL3, MTHFD1L, CANX, CPD, CD276, CKAP4, SLC7A1, DHCR7, LIG3, HADHB, GFM1, EMC1, SHMT2, HPS6, IKBIP, INTS1, ITGA3, ITGAV, ITGB1, LNPEP, MTA2, NCSTN, NSDHL, NT5DC2, NUCB1, PDHX, POLDIP2, PNPT1, QSOX2, RHOG, RRBP1, MRPS31, SEC63, SPTLC1, TMEM214

### Function

The strongest and most coherent interpretation is an endoplasmic-reticulum and organelle-membrane module involved in membrane-protein handling, protein folding, lipid and sterol biology, and coordination between membrane compartments. The primary evidence is the globally corrected enrichment for organelle membrane (GO:CC organelle membrane; q_global = 0.000582), ER membrane and ER subcompartment (GO:CC endoplasmic reticulum membrane; q_global = 0.000748)(GO:CC endoplasmic reticulum subcompartment; q_global = 0.000748), the nuclear-outer-membrane–ER network (GO:CC nuclear outer membrane-endoplasmic reticulum membrane network; q_global = 0.000869), ER (GO:CC endoplasmic reticulum; q_global = 0.00211), membrane (GO:CC membrane; q_global = 0.00407), and organelle subcompartment (GO:CC organelle subcompartment; q_global = 0.0139). The module is therefore better described as a membrane and ER proteostasis program than as a single narrowly defined pathway. Several genes also connect this ER-centered program to cholesterol and sphingolipid synthesis, mitochondrial translation and metabolism, cell adhesion, ion transport, and membrane signaling. Those extensions are biologically plausible but are secondary to the globally significant ER and membrane localization signal. The co-abundance structure may reflect coordinated abundance of proteins residing in, trafficking through, or functionally coupled to the ER and broader endomembrane system.

**Cell composition.** This is not predominantly a plasma-protein or secreted-protein module. The HPA summary identifies only a few secreted genes, while most genes have low tissue specificity and many are detected in both brain and lung. The module is also not strongly blood-cell specific overall: most genes have low immune-cell specificity, although several genes show enhancement in neutrophils or other immune-related cell types. HPA indicates substantial cell-type heterogeneity, including epithelial, hepatocyte, trophoblast, muscle, vascular, mitochondrial, and neutrophil-associated patterns. This diversity argues against interpreting the module simply as a blood-contamination signature, but shared epithelial, vascular, immune, or other cell-type composition across brain and lung samples could contribute to its conservation. Protein detection in both tissues supports biological presence, but it does not by itself establish that the same cell type generates the module in both tissues.

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING supports several established relationships within the module. CANX is linked to EMC1, NCSTN, and SEC63; DHCR7 is linked to NSDHL; GFM1 is linked to MRPS31 and PNPT1; the integrin genes ITGA3, ITGAV, and ITGB1 form a strongly supported interaction group; MTHFD1L is linked to SHMT2; SEC63 is linked to TMEM214; and SHMT2 is linked to SPTLC1. These relationships independently reinforce ER chaperoning and membrane organization, sterol biosynthesis, mitochondrial translation, integrin biology, and one-carbon or sphingolipid-associated metabolism. STRING enrichment also supports membrane, integral membrane, ER, organelle-membrane, and integrin annotations (STRING Keyword Transmembrane helix; within-query FDR = 1.84e-07)(STRING Component Integral component of membrane; within-query FDR = 2.28e-06)(STRING Keyword Membrane; within-query FDR = 1.12e-05)(STRING Component Endoplasmic reticulum membrane; within-query FDR = 5.3e-05)(STRING Component Endoplasmic reticulum; within-query FDR = 9.49e-05)(STRING Component Organelle membrane; within-query FDR = 9.49e-05)(STRING Component Membrane; within-query FDR = 0.00017)(STRING COMPARTMENTS Endoplasmic reticulum; within-query FDR = 0.0028)(STRING Component Integral component of plasma membrane; within-query FDR = 0.006)(STRING Component Endomembrane system; within-query FDR = 0.027)(STRING Component Integrin complex; within-query FDR = 0.0442)(STRING Component Integral component of synaptic membrane; within-query FDR = 0.0442)(STRING Keyword Endoplasmic reticulum; within-query FDR = 0.0478)(STRING Keyword Glycoprotein; within-query FDR = 0.0478)(STRING Keyword Integrin; within-query FDR = 0.0478), but these are supplementary rather than globally corrected primary evidence. hu.MAP provides known complex context for the ITGB1–ITGA3–CD276–LNPEP group (hu.MAP huMAP3_10503.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_10980.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_09336.1), mitochondrial and metabolic context for MTHFD1L, PNPT1, GFM1, and NT5DC2 (hu.MAP huMAP3_13468.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_10525.1), integrin context for ITGB1 and ITGA3 (hu.MAP huMAP3_06815.1), and additional membrane-associated contexts involving CD276, LNPEP, ITGA3, SLC7A1, DHCR7, and QSOX2 (hu.MAP huMAP3_08689.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_04041.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_02887.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_05555.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_08103.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_09105.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_10848.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_06247.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_11349.1)(hu.MAP huMAP3_09592.1). Thus, part of the module recapitulates established physical or complex-level biology, while the full cross-tissue co-abundance assembly is broader than any single known complex.

### Novelty

The module should not be called entirely novel. Many of its relationships and functions are already established, particularly ER chaperoning and translocation, sterol biosynthesis, mitochondrial translation, and integrin complexes. Nevertheless, the specific conserved co-abundance association across brain and lung may represent a new systems-level observation, because the supplied STRING and hu.MAP results show several separate interaction neighborhoods rather than one complete known complex. The absence of a complete matching complex in STRING or hu.MAP would not prove novelty; it only indicates that the observed module is not fully explained by those resources. A defensible conclusion is that the component biology is established, whereas the cross-tissue co-abundance organization and its relative coordination across ER, membrane, mitochondrial, metabolic, and adhesion genes remain potentially novel and require replication and cell-composition controls.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module reflects a conserved cellular state in which ER membrane organization and proteostasis are coordinated with membrane lipid synthesis, glycoprotein processing, mitochondrial metabolic capacity, and cell–matrix adhesion. In this model, CANX, SEC63, EMC1, CKAP4, and related ER proteins support folding, insertion, trafficking, and quality control; DHCR7 and SPTLC1 shape the membrane lipid environment; ITGA3, ITGAV, and ITGB1 connect the membrane system to extracellular-matrix adhesion and signaling; and mitochondrial genes provide metabolic support. Shared epithelial, vascular, or immune-associated cell populations could generate part of this signal in both tissues. This is a mechanistic hypothesis rather than a demonstrated causal pathway and should be tested with cell-resolved data, perturbation of ER or lipid-homeostasis genes, and independent brain–lung cohorts.

## Module 10

**Tissue pair**: brain and lung  
**Genes (25)**: ARHGEF12, ARMC8, ARRB1, COG1, GCC2, GMPR2, GNL1, HDHD2, ITSN2, LRRC57, MARK2, NEMF, NMRAL1, BCKDHB, OTUD7B, PAK1, PHF6, REPS1, SCAF11, SPAST, STARD7, STRN, SYNRG, UBE4A, VPS50

### Function

The most defensible interpretation is a weakly supported intracellular membrane-trafficking, endocytic-recycling, and cytoskeletal-polarity module. This interpretation is based on the co-abundance links involving MARK2, SYNRG, ITSN2, and REPS1, together with annotations for microtubule organization and cell polarity in MARK2, intracellular protein transport in SYNRG, clathrin-dependent endocytosis in ITSN2, and endocytosis and endosomal transport in REPS1. Additional compatible annotations include vesicle and Golgi trafficking for VPS50, COG1, and GCC2, and cytoskeletal or signaling functions for PAK1, ARHGEF12, and STRN. No GO or KEGG term passed global correction, so the functional interpretation remains annotation-led rather than enrichment-led. The absence of a globally significant term does not invalidate the module, but it indicates that its genes do not form a statistically established single pathway under the reported testing framework.

**Cell composition.** This is not a plasma-protein or secreted-protein module: the HPA summary reports no secreted genes. The genes are predominantly broadly expressed, with low tissue specificity, and protein detection is largely present in both brain and lung. The module therefore does not look like a specialized secreted program. Cell-type-enhanced signals are common, including neutrophils, podocytes, microglia, and oligodendrocytes, but overall immune-cell specificity is low. A blood or shared-cell-type contribution cannot be excluded, particularly because several genes show myeloid or neutrophil-associated HPA signals, yet the composition does not support describing the module as mainly blood-cell enriched. The shared brain-lung signal may instead reflect broadly deployed intracellular machinery, with possible admixture from common or contaminating cell populations.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 0 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING provides only one high-confidence relationship within the module, ITSN2–REPS1, and reports no STRING functional enrichment. This means that most module edges are not independently supported by the reported STRING interaction resource and should be treated as co-abundance relationships rather than known physical interactions. hu.MAP provides stronger convergent context: REPS1, ITSN2, and SYNRG co-occur in complexes C1 through C5, while REPS1 and ITSN2 co-occur in additional complexes C6 through C9. These complexes are broadly associated with clathrin-adaptor, endocytic, vesicle-trafficking, and membrane-recycling machinery. The repeated appearance of the same genes in hu.MAP supports a real trafficking-related core, but the partial membership of the module in large complexes does not prove that the entire module is one physical complex.

**STRING interactions (score >= 0.7)**: 1 of 300 gene pairs: ITSN2–REPS1 (0.708)
**hu.MAP 3.0 complexes with >= 2 module members**: huMAP3_12376.1 (REPS1, ITSN2, SYNRG; 3/32); huMAP3_10876.1 (REPS1, ITSN2, SYNRG; 3/40); huMAP3_10762.1 (REPS1, ITSN2, SYNRG; 3/62); huMAP3_12918.1 (REPS1, ITSN2, SYNRG; 3/62); huMAP3_12394.1 (REPS1, ITSN2, SYNRG; 3/98); huMAP3_05849.1 (REPS1, ITSN2; 2/21); huMAP3_09107.1 (REPS1, ITSN2; 2/23); huMAP3_08320.1 (REPS1, ITSN2; 2/24); huMAP3_09403.1 (REPS1, ITSN2; 2/26)

### Novelty

The module should not be declared a new biological pathway or a newly discovered physical complex. Its central trafficking interpretation is consistent with established annotations, the known ITSN2–REPS1 STRING relationship, and repeated hu.MAP membership. The potentially novel observation is the conserved brain-lung co-abundance configuration that links this established trafficking core with MARK2 and several additional genes. However, absence of most module pairs from STRING or hu.MAP does not establish novelty, because those resources are incomplete and measure physical or curated relationships rather than tissue co-abundance. Novelty therefore remains a testable systems-level observation requiring replication in independent cohorts and comparison with existing tissue co-expression resources.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: in both brain and lung, a broadly expressed endocytic and membrane-trafficking program is co-regulated with microtubule polarity and cytoskeletal signaling. MARK2 may coordinate polarity and microtubule state, while ITSN2 and REPS1 may organize clathrin-associated endocytosis and signaling adaptors; SYNRG may connect this machinery to AP-family trafficking. VPS50, COG1, GCC2, and related genes could support recycling and Golgi or endosome routing, while PAK1, ARHGEF12, and STRN could couple trafficking to actin, Rho, and signaling states. This model predicts coordinated changes in endocytic recycling, membrane receptor turnover, and cytoskeletal organization, but it is not proven by the co-abundance data and should be tested with cell-composition adjustment, perturbation, and subcellular or interaction assays.

### Important genes

## Module 11

**Tissue pair**: brain and lung  
**Genes (5)**: ACTR10, CLASP1, FLOT1, MCM3AP, EIF4G3

### Function

This is a small, heterogeneous co-abundance module rather than a statistically established pathway. The strongest biological themes suggested by the individual annotations are cytoskeletal and microtubule organization, vesicle and membrane trafficking, mRNA handling, and translational initiation. MCM3AP connects the cytosolic and nuclear RNA or DNA-associated aspects with CLASP1 and FLOT1, while CLASP1 connects to ACTR10 and therefore provides a possible cytoskeletal or transport link. EIF4G3 supports a translation-initiation interpretation, CLASP1 and ACTR10 support microtubule or intracellular transport functions, and FLOT1 supports membrane-domain and vesicle biology. However, GO/KEGG globally significant GO/KEGG evidence contains no term that passed global correction, so these annotations are hypotheses about the module's biology rather than globally significant enrichment. The lack of enrichment does not invalidate the module, but it argues against assigning it a single definitive pathway label.

**Cell composition.** The module is not composed of plasma or secreted proteins: the HPA secretome annotation reports no secreted genes. All genes have low tissue specificity, which is compatible with broadly expressed cellular machinery rather than a brain- or lung-specific program. The module is not uniformly blood-cell specific, but CLASP1 and FLOT1 show immune-cell enhancement and several genes have neutrophil-related single-cell enrichment; EIF4G3 also has enrichment in neutrophil-related cell populations. This raises the possibility that part of the shared brain-lung signal reflects common cell types, cellular stress, or sample composition, especially blood or immune-cell contribution, rather than a specialized neural-lung mechanism. Protein detection is broadly compatible with the module in brain and generally present in lung, although EIF4G3 is not detected in the reported lung assay and ACTR10 has no reported IHC detection. These HPA observations support biological detectability but do not establish the source of the co-abundance pattern.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 0 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

### STRING and hu.MAP

The STRING evidence is negative at the stated high-confidence threshold: no listed gene pair has a qualifying STRING interaction, and STRING supplementary STRING enrichment is absent. hu.MAP likewise contains no complex with multiple module members. Thus, the co-abundance edges are not explained by a previously catalogued high-confidence protein interaction network or shared hu.MAP complex. The internal network is nevertheless structured: MCM3AP is the central connector, CLASP1 links MCM3AP with ACTR10, and FLOT1 forms a separate connection to MCM3AP. This pattern may represent coordinated regulation or shared cellular context rather than direct physical binding. The lack of STRING and hu.MAP support means that direct complex membership should not be inferred.

**STRING interactions (score >= 0.7)**: 0 of 10 gene pairs.
**hu.MAP 3.0 complexes with >= 2 module members**: none

### Novelty

The module may be a novel co-abundance association, because its gene combination is not represented by a matching high-confidence STRING interaction set or a shared hu.MAP complex. That evidence is supportive but not proof of novelty: absence from these resources can reflect incomplete coverage, context dependence, transient interactions, tissue-specific regulation, or co-regulation without physical binding. The absence of globally significant GO/KEGG enrichment also makes it difficult to claim a newly discovered pathway. The most defensible conclusion is that this is a potentially novel cross-tissue co-abundance pattern whose biological interpretation requires validation in independent cohorts, cell-type-aware analyses, and perturbation or interaction experiments.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the shared brain-lung module reflects a broadly expressed cellular maintenance program that coordinates microtubule organization and intracellular transport with membrane-domain trafficking, RNA export, and translation. MCM3AP may act as the central co-regulated node, linking RNA or nuclear functions with FLOT1-associated membrane trafficking and CLASP1-associated microtubule regulation; CLASP1 and ACTR10 may extend this signal into cytoskeletal or organelle transport. A plausible alternative is that the pattern is partly driven by shared neutrophil or other immune-cell contributions in the tissue samples. This mechanism is explicitly hypothetical because global GO/KEGG evidence is absent, STRING and hu.MAP provide no corroborating relationships, and the genes span several cellular processes.

It was reported to have phosphorylation-dependent DNA-primase activity, which was up-regulated in antigen immunization induced germinal center. This protein was demonstrated to be an acetyltransferase that acetylates…

## Module 12

**Tissue pair**: brain and lung  
**Genes (18)**: ATXN2L, BOD1L1, CHAMP1, CNOT3, CRELD2, GCC1, ISY1, MLST8, ATF7IP, MED22, NCBP3, NCOR1, GATAD2A, PPHLN1, PRRC2C, RBBP6, SUGP1, TMF1

### Function

The module does not support a globally significant GO or KEGG functional assignment: no GO or KEGG term passed global correction. Its gene content nevertheless points toward a heterogeneous nuclear gene-regulatory and RNA-processing program. SUGP1 and ISY1 support spliceosome and RNA-processing biology; NCBP3 supports capped-RNA binding, RNA export and antiviral response; MED22, NCOR1, ATF7IP and GATAD2A support RNA polymerase II regulation, transcriptional repression and chromatin control; RBBP6 and BOD1L1 support RNA processing, ubiquitin-related regulation, DNA damage response and replication-fork biology; and PRRC2C and ATXN2L support stress-granule and post-transcriptional regulation. The network is sparse rather than a densely connected canonical pathway, with SUGP1 acting as the main co-abundance connector and linking several otherwise distinct functional areas. The most defensible interpretation is therefore a shared transcriptional, chromatin and RNA-handling state rather than a single established biochemical pathway. The STRING keyword results for repression (STRING Keyword Repressor; within-query FDR = 0.0052) and isopeptide bond biology (STRING Keyword Isopeptide bond; within-query FDR = 0.0205) are supplementary and do not establish global enrichment.

**Cell composition.** This is not primarily a plasma-protein or secreted-protein module: the genes are overwhelmingly intracellular, with nucleoplasmic, nuclear-speckle, cytosolic or Golgi locations, and only CRELD2 is annotated in the secretome category. The module is also not broadly blood-cell specific. Most genes have low immune-cell specificity, although BOD1L1 is immune-cell enhanced for neutrophils and several genes show cell-type enhancement in neutrophils, B cells or neutrophil progenitors. Many genes are low in tissue specificity and proteins from nearly the whole module are detected in brain and lung, which is consistent with a broadly expressed cellular program shared across tissues. The enrichment of several spermatid-associated annotations and of astrocyte or neuronal annotations appears gene-specific rather than evidence that the whole module represents a single cell type. HPA evidence therefore does not strongly support a blood-contamination explanation, although cell-composition effects cannot be excluded.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 0 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

### STRING and hu.MAP

Known physical or functional support is limited. STRING reports only the high-confidence GCC1–TMF1 relationship among the module pairs. The supplementary STRING keyword associations connect PPHLN1, NCOR1, GATAD2A, ATF7IP, CNOT3 and TMF1 with repression (STRING Keyword Repressor; within-query FDR = 0.0052), and connect a larger subset with isopeptide-bond biology (STRING Keyword Isopeptide bond; within-query FDR = 0.0205); these are within-query STRING findings and are not globally corrected module-level evidence. hu.MAP identifies one complex, with NCBP3 and RBBP6 represented in a much larger RNA-processing-related complex (hu.MAP huMAP3_09943.1). No other module gene has a reported hu.MAP complex in the fact sheet. Thus, the co-abundance structure is not simply recapitulating a well-established physical complex. The limited STRING and hu.MAP overlap suggests that the module may reflect coordinated regulation, shared cellular state or indirect relationships more than stable protein-complex membership.

**STRING interactions (score >= 0.7)**: 1 of 153 gene pairs: GCC1–TMF1 (0.729)
**STRING functional enrichment (supplementary, within-query FDR)**: Keyword Repressor (FDR = 0.0052, 6 genes); Keyword Isopeptide bond (FDR = 0.0205, 10 genes)
**hu.MAP 3.0 complexes with >= 2 module members**: huMAP3_09943.1 (NCBP3, RBBP6; 2/78)

### Novelty

The module is potentially novel as a cross-tissue co-abundance pattern, but the available evidence is insufficient to claim a newly discovered molecular complex or pathway. Supporting novelty are the sparse high-confidence STRING connectivity, the absence of most genes from reported hu.MAP complexes, and the lack of a globally significant GO or KEGG term. However, absence from STRING or hu.MAP does not prove novelty, because those resources emphasize experimentally supported interactions and curated complexes, whereas co-abundance can arise from coordinated expression, cell composition, or condition-dependent regulation. Several component processes are already well established individually, including RNA splicing, transcriptional repression, chromatin remodeling, stress-granule biology, DNA damage response and Golgi trafficking. The appropriate conclusion is that the particular shared brain–lung co-abundance combination is an interesting candidate for further validation, not that all of its biology is unprecedented.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module reflects a shared intracellular gene-expression state in brain and lung in which transcriptional repression and chromatin regulation are coordinated with pre-mRNA processing, RNA export, stress-granule handling and selected DNA-damage or replication responses. SUGP1 may serve as a co-abundance bridge between RNA processing and regulatory or trafficking-associated genes, while MED22, NCOR1, ATF7IP and GATAD2A may define a transcriptional and chromatin-regulatory arm. PRRC2C, NCBP3 and ATXN2L may define a post-transcriptional RNA-handling arm, with RBBP6 and BOD1L1 connecting this state to genome maintenance. This is a mechanistic model generated from annotations and network structure, not an established causal pathway. It should be tested by examining cell-type-adjusted expression, perturbing key regulatory genes, and measuring transcription, splicing, RNA export, stress granules and DNA-damage responses in relevant brain and lung cell models.

### Important genes

#### SUGP1

- **Related proteins** (co-abundance neighbors): MED22 (0.90), CRELD2 (0.86), ISY1 (0.84), RBBP6 (0.83), GCC1 (0.83); STRING partners: none
- **HPA**: Low tissue specificity; Cell type enhanced; blood Low immune cell specificity; location Nucleoplasm; IHC detected in brain, lung

It is a strong candidate for organizing the module's shared RNA-handling signal. A causal role in coordinating the other module functions remains a hypothesis.

Its cell-type-enhanced HPA pattern in astrocytes, Bergmann glia and brain neurons is relevant to the brain component, while its disease associations include neurodegenerative disease and stroke-related phenotypes.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports MED22 as a Mediator component involved in RNA polymerase II transcriptional regulation. - **Why important**: NCOR1 is a major transcriptional-repression regulator with extensive literature in the module. It connects to TMF1 and BOD1L1 and is present in both supplementary STRING keyword groups (STRING Keyword Repressor; within-query FDR = 0.0052) and (STRING Keyword Isopeptide bond; within-query FDR = 0.0205). Its functions in nuclear-receptor corepression, chromatin condensation and repression of transcription provide a mechanistic counterpart to MED22-mediated transcriptional regulation. It also has disease associations involving lung and other cancers.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports NCOR1 as a corepressor that regulates transcription through chromatin-associated mechanisms. Its module membership is consistent with a repressive gene-regulatory state. The hypothesis that NCOR1 coordinates RNA processing or genome-maintenance genes in this module requires direct interaction or perturbation evidence.

It has extensive literature and disease associations that include neurodegenerative and retinal disorders. Its HPA tissue enhancement in bone marrow and cell-type enhancement in platelets should be considered when evaluating sample composition.

It also has disease associations involving neurodegenerative and neurodevelopmental phenotypes.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports PRRC2C as a cytoplasmic RNA and stress-granule regulator. Its position suggests that the module may couple nuclear gene expression with cytoplasmic RNA handling. Whether this coupling is a true shared mechanism in brain and lung, rather than correlated expression of broadly expressed genes, remains a hypothesis.

## Module 13

**Tissue pair**: brain and lung  
**Genes (8)**: BECN1, HSPA13, MAIP1, NDUFAF4, RAB10, RAB21, HARS2, SLC7A11

### Function

This is a heterogeneous cellular-maintenance module rather than a module with a single formally enriched pathway. No GO/KEGG term passed global correction. The network links intracellular membrane trafficking through RAB21 and RAB10, cystine and glutamate transport through SLC7A11, autophagy and vesicle-trafficking control through BECN1, mitochondrial protein insertion and calcium handling through MAIP1, mitochondrial complex I assembly through NDUFAF4, mitochondrial translation through HARS2, and protein quality control through HSPA13. RAB21 is the central network connector, linking the trafficking-related genes with SLC7A11, BECN1, and the MAIP1-centered mitochondrial/proteostasis branch. Thus, the most defensible interpretation is coordinated cellular homeostasis involving vesicle traffic, nutrient and redox handling, mitochondrial function, autophagy, and proteostasis. This is a functional interpretation of the gene annotations and network structure, not evidence of a globally enriched pathway.

**Cell composition.** The module does not appear predominantly plasma-derived, secreted, or blood-cell enriched. HPA reports low tissue specificity for most genes, low immune-cell specificity for nearly all genes, and no frequent enriched blood-cell category. Most proteins are detected in both brain and lung, supporting broad cellular presence rather than a brain-exclusive or lung-exclusive protein set. The module nevertheless has substantial cell-type heterogeneity: several genes have cell-type-enhanced expression, including patterns associated with oocytes, epithelial or specialized secretory cells, glial cells, corticotrophs, plasma cells, and neutrophils. SLC7A11 is tissue-enhanced in brain and is associated with several neural and glial cell types. One gene is classified as secreted in the HPA summary, but the overall module is not a secreted-protein module. The shared signal could therefore reflect conserved cellular states or shared cell populations in the sampled tissues, rather than a tissue-specific organ function. HPA does not support a simple blood-contamination explanation.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 0 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

### STRING and hu.MAP

The available STRING evidence identifies one high-confidence known relationship, between BECN1 and SLC7A11. This connects autophagy regulation with cystine and glutamate transport and gives biological support to part of the module's homeostasis interpretation. STRING reports no supplementary functional-enrichment terms. The co-abundance network also contains RAB21-centered relationships that are not represented as known STRING partners in the fact sheet, so co-abundance should not be treated as direct physical interaction. hu.MAP contains no complex with multiple module members. Consequently, the module is not explained by a recognized multiprotein complex; it appears to combine several cellular systems connected by coordinated abundance patterns, with only a limited subset already supported by curated interaction evidence.

**STRING interactions (score >= 0.7)**: 1 of 28 gene pairs: BECN1–SLC7A11 (0.835)
**hu.MAP 3.0 complexes with >= 2 module members**: none

### Novelty

This module should be regarded as a potentially novel co-abundance organization, but not as proven discovery of a new molecular complex or mechanism. The combination of a RAB21-centered trafficking branch, the BECN1-SLC7A11 autophagy and cystine-transport relationship, and a MAIP1-linked mitochondrial branch is not represented by a corresponding hu.MAP complex and is only sparsely represented in STRING. That supports novelty of the observed cross-system co-abundance pattern. However, absence from STRING or hu.MAP alone cannot establish novelty: database coverage is incomplete, co-abundance does not prove physical binding, and some constituent genes have extensive prior literature. The strongest novelty claim is therefore that this particular conserved brain-lung association and its internal organization are candidate findings requiring replication and independent validation.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module reflects a conserved cellular stress-adaptation program in which RAB21-mediated membrane trafficking coordinates nutrient and metabolite handling with organelle quality control. SLC7A11 could support cystine import and glutathione-related redox protection, while BECN1 could connect that state to autophagy and lysosomal trafficking. In parallel, MAIP1, NDUFAF4, and HARS2 could reflect preservation of mitochondrial protein insertion, respiratory-chain assembly, and mitochondrial translation, with HSPA13 contributing proteostasis. Under this model, the shared brain-lung co-abundance signal arises from coordinated adaptation to oxidative, metabolic, or proteotoxic stress in conserved cell populations. This is a mechanistic hypothesis, not a demonstrated causal pathway; the co-abundance edges and the limited STRING support do not establish directionality or direct physical coupling.

## Module 14

**Tissue pair**: brain and lung  
**Genes (34)**: HSPA5, CD44, NCAPG2, FUS, GANAB, HNRNPH1, HNRNPK, HSP90AA1, HSPA8, HYOU1, KPNA2, IMPDH2, TMPO, TMPO, MCM2, MCM4, MCM5, MCM6, MCM7, MMP14, MOGS, MSH2, NASP, PCNA, PDIA4, PRKDC, PTBP1, SF3B3, SMC2, TNPO1, SNRNP200, XPO1, CSE1L, XRCC6

### Function

The module is best interpreted as a nuclear genome-maintenance and cell-proliferation program centered on DNA replication, replication-fork activity, DNA recombination, chromosome organization, and DNA-damage repair. The strongest primary evidence is the MCM complex and CMG complex (GO:CC MCM complex; q_global = 1.51e-06) (GO:CC CMG complex; q_global = 6.85e-05), DNA replication and cell-cycle pathways (4 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 0.000432–0.0362; see the table), replication initiation (4 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 6.85e-05–0.00355; see the table), helicase activities (9 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 9.45e-05–0.0103; see the table), and double-strand-break and recombinational repair (6 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 1.67e-05–0.0175; see the table). The module also contains a secondary stress and protein-homeostasis component, supported by cellular stress (GO:BP cellular response to stress; q_global = 8.84e-05) (GO:BP regulation of cellular response to stress; q_global = 0.00793), endoplasmic-reticulum processing (KEGG Protein processing in endoplasmic reticulum - Homo sapiens (human); q_global = 0.00189), and chaperone activity (GO:MF ATP-dependent protein folding chaperone; q_global = 0.0114). Nucleocytoplasmic transport (GO:MF nucleocytoplasmic carrier activity; q_global = 0.035) and RNA-processing proteins appear to connect the replication and stress components. Thus, the most defensible interpretation is a conserved proliferative and genome-integrity module, with associated proteostasis and nuclear-transport support rather than a tissue-specialized brain or lung pathway.

**Cell composition.** This is not primarily a plasma or secreted-protein module: the HPA summary identifies only a minority of secreted genes, and most characterized members are intracellular nuclear, cytosolic, or endoplasmic-reticulum proteins. It is also not strongly blood-cell specific by the HPA blood-cell classification. However, many genes are cell-type enhanced in erythrocyte progenitors, monocyte progenitors, and megakaryocyte progenitors, and several show tissue enhancement in bone marrow or lymphoid tissue. Most genes have low tissue specificity and the proteins are broadly detected in brain and lung. This pattern is compatible with a broadly expressed housekeeping or proliferation-associated program, potentially influenced by shared proliferative cell populations or blood-derived material in the samples. The progenitor enrichment should therefore be considered an important compositional caveat, not evidence that the module is intrinsically brain- or lung-specific. MCM2 has no protein detection in the listed brain and lung samples, and several other individual detection differences exist, so module-level co-abundance should not be equated with uniform protein presence in every cell type.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 60 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING provides substantial support that the module contains established molecular systems rather than an arbitrary collection of correlated genes. The MCM proteins form a dense, highly supported interaction group, with links to PCNA, NASP, SMC2, and DNA-repair proteins. The MCM complex is independently recovered as a STRING functional component (STRING Component MCM complex; within-query FDR = 8.85e-06) (STRING COMPARTMENTS MCM complex; within-query FDR = 1.96e-05), while telomeric-region, DNA-binding, nucleic-acid-binding, and ATP-dependent categories provide supplementary concordance (4 supplementary STRING terms; within-query FDR 3.82e-06–1.09e-05). A second established interaction neighborhood contains HSPA5, HSPA8, HYOU1, PDIA4, GANAB, and HSP90AA1, consistent with chaperoning and ER protein-folding support. A third contains FUS, HNRNPH1, HNRNPK, PTBP1, SF3B3, and SNRNP200, consistent with RNA processing and nuclear ribonucleoprotein biology. hu.MAP independently identifies repeated complexes containing the MCM proteins (hu.MAP huMAP3_05529.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_14758.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08554.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_11832.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_12180.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_06061.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_07113.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_11119.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09860.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_00154.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09466.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10862.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_02602.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09238.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08848.1), condensin-related SMC2-NCAPG2 complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_03084.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_14385.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09855.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_11457.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_14663.1), nuclear transport complexes involving XPO1, CSE1L, PRKDC, and TNPO1 (hu.MAP huMAP3_09592.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09042.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_07170.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_06791.1), an hnRNP complex (hu.MAP huMAP3_14523.1), ER proteostasis complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_06775.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09327.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_07455.1), and a spliceosomal complex (hu.MAP huMAP3_08980.1). These results indicate that the co-abundance module integrates several known molecular neighborhoods, with the MCM replication machinery as its clearest structural core.

### Novelty

The module should not be claimed as a newly discovered molecular complex. Its central relationships, especially the MCM assembly, replication machinery, DNA-repair associations, chaperone network, RNA-processing network, and nuclear-transport relationships, are already represented in STRING and hu.MAP. Absence of some individual genes from STRING or hu.MAP does not establish novelty. A potentially novel aspect is the reproducible co-abundance of these established systems in one conserved brain-lung module, particularly the coupling of replication and genome-integrity machinery to ER stress, chaperoning, RNA processing, and nuclear transport. Establishing that as a new biological finding would require replication in independent cohorts, control for cell composition and proliferation, and ideally perturbation or spatial evidence. The appropriate conclusion is therefore a potentially novel tissue-shared co-abundance organization of largely known biology, not a novel protein complex or pathway.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module reflects a conserved response of proliferating or regeneration-associated cells in brain and lung in which MCM-mediated replication is coordinated with PCNA-supported DNA synthesis, mismatch and double-strand-break repair, chromosome organization, and nuclear trafficking. Increased replication or replication stress could recruit chaperone and ER-quality-control machinery, including HSPA5, HYOU1, PDIA4, and GANAB, while FUS and hnRNP proteins help maintain RNA processing during the altered cell state. The progenitor-enriched HPA pattern suggests that shared cellular composition or proliferative state may drive much of the signal. This hypothesis predicts that module abundance will track proliferation and replication-stress markers, and that the association will weaken after careful adjustment for progenitor, immune, and blood-derived cell fractions.

- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports PCNA as a replication processivity and DNA-repair coordinator and as a bridge between the replication and repair portions of the module. Hypothesis: PCNA may help couple replication activity to repair and RNA-processing neighborhoods in the shared module.

The module does not establish a disease mechanism in brain or lung. Hypothesis: FUS may coordinate transcript processing with repair and replication-associated nuclear stress in the conserved module.

## Module 15

**Tissue pair**: brain and lung  
**Genes (32)**: CENPF, ENY2, EP300, GPATCH8, IMPDH1, KRI1, MMTAG2, PHF14, PPIG, PSMD13, RBM12, RBM15, RBM19, RBM27, RBM7, RB1, RCOR1, SCAF8, SDE2, SNRNP40, SMNDC1, SREK1, STAG2, STEEP1, DNTTIP2, GTF3C4, TMEM263, UBR5, WAPL, WDR33, ZC3H4, ZC3H14

### Function

The strongest interpretation is a conserved nuclear RNA metabolism and gene-expression module. Primary enrichment supports RNA binding (GO:MF RNA binding; q_global = 3.98e-05), nucleic-acid binding (GO:MF nucleic acid binding; q_global = 0.000216), nucleoplasm and nuclear localization (GO:CC nucleoplasm; q_global = 0.000281) (GO:CC nuclear lumen; q_global = 0.000293) (GO:CC nucleus; q_global = 0.0011), RNA biosynthesis (GO:BP RNA biosynthetic process; q_global = 0.00233), nuclear speckles and ribonucleoprotein granules (GO:CC nuclear speck; q_global = 0.00282) (GO:CC nuclear ribonucleoprotein granule; q_global = 0.00698), RNA splicing and processing (GO:BP RNA splicing; q_global = 0.00339) (GO:BP RNA processing; q_global = 0.014), and broader nucleic-acid metabolic and biosynthetic processes (5 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 0.00409–0.0205; see the table). The convergence of these independently corrected terms indicates coordinated regulation of nuclear RNA handling, including pre-mRNA splicing, RNA surveillance, RNA maturation, transcriptional control, and possibly ribosome-related RNA processing. The signal is not best described as a single narrowly defined pathway: it is a multifunctional nuclear gene-expression program. Cell-cycle and chromatin-related genes in the module, including RB1, EP300, STAG2, WAPL, CENPF, RCOR1, and UBR5, suggest coupling of RNA regulation to transcriptional control, chromatin organization, genome maintenance, and cell proliferation. These latter interpretations are supported by individual gene annotations and network context rather than by a distinct globally enriched cell-cycle term.

**Cell composition.** This is not a plasma-protein or secreted-protein module: the HPA summary reports no secreted genes, and most proteins are detected in both brain and lung. The module is dominated by broadly expressed, nuclear or nucleolar proteins, with low tissue specificity for most genes and low immune-cell specificity for nearly all genes. Therefore, a blood-contamination explanation is not the leading interpretation. Some members show enrichment in bone marrow, testis, retina, lymphoid tissue, or particular cell types such as neutrophils, progenitors, spermatocytes, or retinal cells, so variation in cellular composition could contribute in particular samples. CENPF is not detected in brain or lung by the stated HPA protein evidence, whereas most other measured proteins are detected in both tissues. Overall, the shared module more plausibly reflects a broadly deployed nuclear RNA-processing program, potentially modulated by shared proliferative or stromal cell states, than a tissue-specific secreted or blood-cell program.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 18 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING provides supplementary support for nuclear localization, nucleic-acid binding, RNA binding, RNA processing, nuclear speckles, and related functions (15 supplementary STRING terms; within-query FDR 1.25e-05–0.0012), but these STRING enrichment results are not primary global enrichment evidence. The high-confidence STRING relationships include EP300 with RB1 and STAG2, STAG2 with WAPL, PPIG with SNRNP40, SDE2 with SNRNP40, SCAF8 with WDR33, and KRI1 with RBM19. These links connect transcriptional and chromatin regulation, cohesin biology, spliceosomal activity, and nucleolar RNA processing. hu.MAP independently shows repeated membership of PPIG, ZC3H14, SREK1, and GPATCH8 in several RNA-processing and splicing-related complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_10789.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_11073.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09943.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09226.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09637.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09621.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_05740.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08302.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08467.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_06097.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_10564.1). WAPL and STAG2 recur in cohesin complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_00229.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_14972.1), while SDE2, SREK1, and MMTAG2 occur in another complex context (hu.MAP huMAP3_05807.1), and STEEP1 with RBM27 in (hu.MAP huMAP3_13593.1). Thus, some module relationships are already known physical or complex-level associations, especially around spliceosome-associated factors and cohesin. However, the measured co-abundance network is not simply a reconstruction of the known physical interactome: many high-weight module edges lack STRING support, and the network contains only a limited subset of possible STRING-supported pairs. The module therefore appears to combine known molecular subassemblies with coordinated expression relationships that may reflect shared regulation, cell state, or indirect functional coupling.

### Novelty

The module should not be called a wholly new molecular complex. Several of its relationships and functional groupings are already represented in STRING and hu.MAP, particularly spliceosome-associated proteins, nuclear speckle factors, RNA-binding proteins, and cohesin components. At the same time, the brain-lung conserved co-abundance organization itself may be a novel systems-level observation because the databases do not establish this specific cross-tissue co-abundance module, and many of its network edges are not documented physical interactions. Absence from STRING or hu.MAP is not evidence of novelty by itself; it can reflect incomplete interaction coverage, indirect regulation, shared expression, or context dependence. The defensible novelty claim is therefore that this particular conserved brain-lung co-abundance configuration, and its combination of RNA-processing, transcriptional, chromatin, and cohesin-associated genes, merits validation as a cross-tissue expression program rather than being presented as a newly discovered protein complex.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module represents a shared nuclear RNA quality-control and gene-expression state maintained across brain and lung. In this model, RNA-binding and splicing factors in nuclear speckles coordinate transcript processing and surveillance; nucleolar factors support ribosomal RNA maturation; transcriptional and chromatin regulators tune the production and processing of those transcripts; and cohesin-associated proteins help couple transcriptional organization to chromosome and cell-cycle state. The conserved co-abundance pattern could arise because these components are jointly induced or stabilized in cells requiring high RNA-processing capacity, or because the module tracks a shared proliferative, repair, or stress-responsive state. This hypothesis predicts coordinated changes in RNA splicing, RNA stability, transcriptional elongation, and chromatin organization, but co-abundance alone does not establish direct biochemical cooperation or causal regulation.

Members of the SPEN (Split-end) family of proteins, including RBM15, have repressor function in several signaling pathways and may bind to RNA through interaction with spliceosome components (Hiriart et al., 2005 [PubMed 16129689]).[supplied by OMIM, Feb 2009].
- **Diseases (Open Targets)**: neurodegenerative disease (0.526); lung adenocarcinoma (0.219); esophageal squamous cell carcinoma (0.209); osteosarcoma (0.209); hepatocellular carcinoma (0.206)

- **Why important**: RBM15 is a central network member with connections to RBM12, KRI1, RB1, and PHF14. It is present across all primary enrichment themes (18 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 3.98e-05–0.0205; see the table), is located in the nucleoplasm, is tissue enhanced in bone marrow, and has established annotations for alternative mRNA splicing, RNA methylation, myeloid differentiation, and megakaryocyte differentiation. Its extensive disease and literature association makes it a biologically informative bridge between RNA processing, hematopoietic regulation, and the shared brain-lung module.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports RBM15 as an RNA-binding and splicing-related regulatory factor embedded in the module, with links to RNA methylation and myeloid-cell biology. The hypothesis that RBM15 helps coordinate the broader conserved module across brain and lung is plausible from its network position and annotations, but is not demonstrated by the co-abundance data alone.

## Module 16

**Tissue pair**: brain and lung  
**Genes (7)**: CIAO1, AHCTF1, RRP12, PDCD11, SEH1L, TBL3, ZC3HAV1

### Function

No GO/KEGG term passed global correction, so the module should not be assigned a formally enriched pathway. Its internal structure nevertheless points to a coherent nuclear and RNA-biogenesis program. PDCD11, TBL3, and RRP12 form a densely interconnected STRING-supported group associated with nucleolar rRNA processing and small-subunit ribosome biogenesis. AHCTF1 and SEH1L connect the module to nuclear-pore organization and nucleocytoplasmic transport, with support from the shared hu.MAP complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_07492.1) and (hu.MAP huMAP3_09710.1). CIAO1 contributes a cytosolic iron-sulfur assembly and protein-maturation component, while ZC3HAV1 contributes antiviral RNA surveillance and innate immune regulation. Overall, the most defensible interpretation is a broadly cellular nuclear-maintenance module combining ribosome production, nuclear transport, protein maturation, and RNA-linked antiviral control rather than a tissue-specialized brain-lung pathway.

**Cell composition.** The module is not composed of secreted proteins, and most genes have low tissue specificity. Protein detection in brain and lung is common among the genes with available measurements, consistent with broadly expressed intracellular proteins. HPA nevertheless indicates a blood and immune contribution: several genes are enhanced in neutrophils or related myeloid populations, RRP12 is enriched in bone marrow and multiple myeloid cell types, and ZC3HAV1 is enhanced in bone marrow and several immune or endometrial cell types. AHCTF1 is specifically enhanced in neutrophils. Thus, the shared brain-lung signal may partly reflect ubiquitous housekeeping biology and may also be influenced by shared blood or immune-cell content in the samples. It is not well explained by circulating secreted proteins.

**GO/KEGG enrichment**: 0 terms pass global BH (q_global < 0.05).

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING supports the nucleolar RNA-processing core through the PDCD11-RRP12, PDCD11-TBL3, and RRP12-TBL3 relationships, and supports the AHCTF1-SEH1L relationship. The observed STRING-supported links are concentrated in two internal groups rather than forming a uniformly connected network. hu.MAP independently places AHCTF1 and SEH1L together in two nuclear-pore-related complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_07492.1) and (hu.MAP huMAP3_09710.1), whose other members include multiple nucleoporins. This supports a real nuclear-pore or nucleocytoplasmic-transport connection, but only a subset of each complex is represented in the module. There was no STRING functional-enrichment result, and no additional hu.MAP complex with multiple module members was supplied. These resources therefore support selected physical or functional relationships, not a complete experimentally established complex containing all module genes.

**STRING interactions (score >= 0.7)**: 4 of 21 gene pairs: AHCTF1–SEH1L (0.998); PDCD11–RRP12 (0.984); PDCD11–TBL3 (0.996); RRP12–TBL3 (0.953)
**hu.MAP 3.0 complexes with >= 2 module members**: huMAP3_07492.1 (AHCTF1, SEH1L; 2/10); huMAP3_09710.1 (AHCTF1, SEH1L; 2/13)

### Novelty

The module may be a new co-abundance observation, but the underlying biology is not wholly novel. The PDCD11-TBL3-RRP12 relationships and the AHCTF1-SEH1L nuclear-pore association are already represented by STRING or hu.MAP, and the individual gene functions are established in the supplied annotations. What may be novel is their coordinated abundance across brain and lung, especially the combination of nucleolar RNA processing, nuclear-pore components, CIAO1, and antiviral RNA regulation. However, absence of the full seven-gene pattern from STRING or hu.MAP would not by itself establish novelty. The lack of globally significant GO/KEGG enrichment also limits claims that this is a newly defined pathway. The appropriate conclusion is a potentially novel cross-tissue co-abundance configuration built partly from known molecular relationships, requiring replication and adjustment for blood-cell composition.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module reflects coordinated activity of a nuclear homeostasis program in shared cells or shared sample components of brain and lung. A nucleolar arm involving PDCD11, TBL3, and RRP12 may support rRNA processing and ribosome biogenesis, while an adjacent nuclear-pore arm involving AHCTF1 and SEH1L may couple this state to nucleocytoplasmic transport and nuclear organization. CIAO1 may connect the state to iron-sulfur protein maturation, and ZC3HAV1 may indicate an interferon- or antiviral-responsive context. The immune-cell enrichment, particularly the neutrophil and myeloid signal, raises the alternative hypothesis that the apparent cross-tissue module is partly driven by shared blood or immune-cell abundance rather than coordinated regulation within neurons or lung parenchymal cells. This should be tested with cell-composition adjustment, cell-resolved expression, and independent cohorts.

### Important genes

It has substantial literature coverage and disease associations including systemic lupus erythematosus, insomnia, back pain, and neoplasm.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports PDCD11 as a central nucleolar RNA-processing component and a plausible anchor of the module. The cross-tissue coordination of PDCD11 with the other module genes is an association, not proof that PDCD11 drives the module. A role in linking ribosome biogenesis to nuclear organization remains a hypothesis.

It also has disease associations involving Huntington disease, pulmonary fibrosis, infection, and neoplasm, with substantial literature coverage.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports TBL3 as an interaction-oriented component of rRNA processing and ribosome biogenesis. The data do not establish whether RRP12 is functionally induced in brain or lung tissue or is carried by infiltrating or circulating cells. Its role as a bridge between ribosome biogenesis and immune-cell composition is therefore a hypothesis.

It is also enhanced in neutrophils, which makes it relevant both to the nuclear-transport interpretation and to the possibility of blood-cell influence. - **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports AHCTF1 as a nuclear-pore and nuclear-organization component with a known relationship to SEH1L. Its co-abundance with nucleolar genes could indicate coordinated nuclear maintenance, but the module does not prove such functional coupling. The neutrophil signal means that a cellular-composition explanation must be considered before assigning the pattern to resident brain or lung cells.

It connects to CIAO1 in the module network, is enhanced in bone marrow and several immune-related cell types, and has the largest literature presence among the supplied genes. Its disease associations include cancer, hypothyroidism, neurodegenerative disease, autoimmune disease, and Hashimoto thyroiditis.
- **Conclusion**: Established evidence supports ZC3HAV1 as an antiviral RNA-surveillance and innate-immune regulator. Its inclusion suggests that immune or inflammatory context may accompany the nuclear-maintenance program, but it does not establish that antiviral signaling caused the entire module. A coordinated response involving RNA processing, nuclear organization, and antiviral defense is a plausible hypothesis that requires direct perturbation or cell-resolved validation.

## Module 17

**Tissue pair**: brain and lung  
**Genes (22)**: CLPB, DAGLB, DOCK9, HLTF, KGD4, CHCHD3, PDHB, PHIP, PTCD3, UPF3B, RIF1, MRPL4, MRPL11, MRPL19, MRPL45, MRPL50, MRPL55, MRPS22, MRPS23, MRPS27, SRC, UNK

### Function

The primary evidence supports a mitochondrial translation module. The strongest coherent signal is mitochondrial and organellar ribosome structure and function, including large- and small-subunit components, mitochondrial translation, mitochondrial gene expression, and ribosomal protein activity (7 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 2.36e-13–1.13e-07; see the table), (GO:BP mitochondrial gene expression; q_global = 1.59e-06), (GO:CC organellar large ribosomal subunit; q_global = 1.21e-05) (GO:CC mitochondrial large ribosomal subunit; q_global = 1.21e-05) (GO:MF structural constituent of ribosome; q_global = 2.8e-05), (GO:BP translation; q_global = 8.76e-05) (GO:BP protein biosynthetic process; q_global = 8.76e-05), (7 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 0.000147–0.00294; see the table). Additional globally significant terms place the module in mitochondrial membranes, the mitochondrial envelope, and mitochondrial protein-containing complexes (GO:CC mitochondrial protein-containing complex; q_global = 2.45e-08), (GO:CC mitochondrial inner membrane; q_global = 7.93e-07), (GO:CC organelle inner membrane; q_global = 1.78e-06) (GO:CC mitochondrial envelope; q_global = 8.03e-06), (GO:CC mitochondrial membrane; q_global = 3.68e-05), (GO:CC organelle envelope; q_global = 0.000112), (GO:CC mitochondrial matrix; q_global = 0.00117) (GO:CC mitochondrion; q_global = 0.0019). Thus, this is best interpreted as a conserved mitochondrial protein-synthesis and respiratory-organelle maintenance program rather than as a general cytosolic translation module. The peripheral presence of PDHB and KGD4 is compatible with mitochondrial metabolic coupling, while CHCHD3 is compatible with inner-membrane and cristae organization. The co-abundance graph is relatively sparse but contains a dense, biologically coherent mitoribosomal core, with MRPL45 linking that core to several metabolic, membrane, and regulatory genes. The GO and KEGG results are primary evidence because they passed global correction; STRING enrichment is supplementary corroboration only.

**Cell composition.** The module does not look like a plasma-protein, secreted-protein, or blood-cell contamination module. HPA reports broad tissue distribution for most genes, only one secreted gene, and predominantly low immune-cell specificity. Protein detection is widespread in both brain and lung, supporting a shared cellular mitochondrial program rather than a signal restricted to one tissue. A minority of genes show cell-type enhancement or tissue enrichment, including neuronal, epithelial, muscle, reproductive, endothelial, and immune-associated contexts, so mixed cellular composition may contribute to co-abundance. However, the dominant composition is broadly expressed intracellular mitochondrial and translation machinery, not circulating or blood-cell-enriched proteins. The shared signal could therefore reflect common metabolically active cells in brain and lung, with cell-composition effects remaining a possible contributor.

### STRING and hu.MAP

STRING provides strong, mutually reinforcing known relationships among the mitoribosomal genes, especially the MRPL and MRPS proteins and PTCD3 (9 supplementary STRING terms; within-query FDR 2.08e-13–1.26e-11), (STRING Process Mitochondrial translation; within-query FDR = 3.45e-10), (STRING COMPARTMENTS Mitochondrial large ribosomal subunit; within-query FDR = 2.67e-08) (STRING Component Mitochondrial large ribosomal subunit; within-query FDR = 2.88e-08). The listed high-confidence pairwise relationships form a dense interaction backbone for the mitoribosomal core, meaning that much of the module recapitulates established molecular connectivity rather than representing arbitrary co-expression. STRING also connects part of the module to mitochondrial protein complexes and the inner membrane (STRING Component Mitochondrial protein-containing complex; within-query FDR = 4.31e-11), (STRING COMPARTMENTS Mitochondrial protein complex; within-query FDR = 4.98e-09) (STRING Component Mitochondrial inner membrane; within-query FDR = 5.99e-09). hu.MAP independently places the large-subunit genes MRPL11, MRPL19, MRPL4, MRPL50, and MRPL55 together across several mitochondrial ribosome complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_06439.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_06550.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_07939.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08458.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09113.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09483.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_06589.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_04652.1), and places MRPS22, MRPS23, MRPS27, and PTCD3 together in small-subunit-related complexes (hu.MAP huMAP3_05954.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_05807.1). PDHB and KGD4 are jointly represented in a pyruvate-dehydrogenase-related complex (hu.MAP huMAP3_12248.1). These results establish that the central module is embedded in known mitochondrial ribosome and metabolic complexes. The absence of STRING or hu.MAP support for some peripheral links does not invalidate them as co-abundance relationships, but it makes those links less mechanistically established.

### Novelty

The module is unlikely to be a newly discovered mitochondrial ribosome or mitochondrial translation complex: its central genes, functions, STRING relationships, and hu.MAP memberships are already well established (7 globally significant GO/KEGG terms, q_global 2.36e-13–1.13e-07; see the table), (15 supplementary STRING terms; within-query FDR 2.08e-13–2.88e-08), (hu.MAP huMAP3_06439.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_06550.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_07939.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_08458.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09113.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_09483.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_06589.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_04652.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_05954.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_05807.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_02720.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_12248.1) (hu.MAP huMAP3_00983.1). The potentially novel observation is the conserved co-abundance organization of this known machinery across brain and lung, particularly the association of a mitoribosomal core with MRPL45-linked metabolic, membrane, and regulatory genes. That tissue-pair conservation may provide a useful systems-level finding, but it should not be called a wholly new biological pathway. Absence of an individual edge from STRING or hu.MAP is not evidence of novelty. A definitive claim of publication-level novelty would require a systematic comparison with prior tissue co-abundance studies and datasets, which is not supplied here.

**Hypothesis.** Hypothesis: the module represents coordinated control of mitochondrial gene expression and respiratory capacity in shared metabolically active cell populations of brain and lung. The mitoribosomal core may increase or decrease together with mitochondrial membrane and cristae organization through CHCHD3, while PDHB and KGD4 couple mitochondrial translation demand to pyruvate entry and tricarboxylic-acid-cycle metabolism. MRPL45 may act as a co-abundance bridge between the ribosomal machinery and these metabolic or membrane-associated components. This hypothesis predicts that module activity will track mitochondrial translation, respiratory-chain protein production, cristae integrity, and cellular energy demand, but the co-abundance data alone do not establish direct regulation or physical binding for the peripheral genes.
